The Bernstein-von Mises theorem and efficiency for semiparametric inference in multivariate Hawkes processes
Maël Duverger, Judith Rousseau

Abstract
In this paper, we study semiparametric inference for linear multivariate Hawkes processes, a class of point processes widely used to describe self and mutually exciting phenomena. We establish a convolution theorem giving the best limiting distribution for a regular estimator of smooth functional. Then, in the Bayesian setting, we prove a semiparametric Bernstein-von Mises (BvM) theorem for nonparametric random series priors. We apply this result to histogram and wavelet based priors. Taken together, the convolution and BvM theorems show that, from a frequentist point of view, semiparametric Bayesian procedures have asymptotically the optimal behavior. Deriving the BvM property for random series priors led us to prove L 2 L_{2} posterior contraction, complementing for these priors the results of Donnet, Rivoirard and Rousseau (2020).

Contents

1
Introduction

1.1
Hawkes processes

Multivariate Hawkes processes are point processes introduced by Alan G. Hawkes in 1971
[
34
]
. Let
N
be a marked point process on
\mathbb{R}
with marks in
[K]:=\{1,...,K\}
. For a real Borel set
A
,
N(A)
is the number of occurrences in
A
,
N^{k}(A)
is the number of occurrences in
A
with mark
k
and for
t\geq 0
,
N_{t}^{k}:=N^{k}([0,t])
. Let
\mathcal{G}=\{\mathcal{G}_{t},t\in\mathbb{R}\}
be the internal history, the stochastic (predictable) intensity of the process is given by

\lambda_{t}^{k}=\underset{\Delta\searrow 0}{\lim}\frac{\mathbb{P}\big(N^{k}(]t,t+\Delta])=1|\mathcal{G}_{t-}\big)}{\Delta},\hskip 8.5359ptk\in[K].

For a linear multivariate Hawkes process
N
with marks in
[K]
, we have

\lambda_{t}^{k}(\nu_{k},\mathbf{h}_{k})=\nu_{k}+\sum_{l=1}^{K}\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h_{l,k}(t-u)dN_{u}^{l}\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 8.5359ptk\in[K],

(1.1)

\nu_{k}>0
is the background rate and the functions
\mathbf{h}_{k}=(h_{l,k})_{l\in[K]}
, called interaction functions, are non negative and supported on
[0,+\infty[
. The parameters of a Hawkes process are thus
f=(\nu,\mathbf{h})
with
\nu=(\nu_{1},...,\nu_{K})\in]0,+\infty[^{K}
and
\mathbf{h}
is the
K\times K
matrix whose
k
-th column is
\mathbf{h}_{k}=(h_{1,k},...,h_{K,k})
.

Hawkes processes are used in a wide range of fields such as seismology
[
50
,
63
]
, genomics
[
30
,
54
]
, neuroscience
[
6
,
52
]
, finance
[
1
,
2
,
24
]
and cyber security
[
3
,
35
]
.

There is a vast literature on statistical inference in Hawkes processes. In parametric models, starting from the seminal works
[
49
]
and
[
39
]
(chapter 4), several extensions and generalisations have been proposed, see for instance
[
8
,
9
]
and the references therein. In particular, nonparametric estimation was first studied in
[
54
]
and then a Lasso-type estimator was proposed in
[
31
]
. Shortly after, an
edge screening
approach, with a low computational cost, to recover the graph of interaction was proposed in
[
16
]
. In the Bayesian setting, nonparametric posterior contraction was established in
[
21
,
58
]
, extensions to settings where
K
is high dimensional have been recently proposed in
[
56
]
while variational Bayes methods for Hawkes processes have been studied in
[
59
]
, see also references therein such as
[
64
]
.

However, none of these works address the important question of semiparametric inference. Certain functionals are of special interest in the analysis of a Hawkes process such as the background rate
\nu_{k}
or the interaction matrix
\rho
defined by
\rho_{l,k}=\int h_{l,k}(x)dx
. Indeed, in the univariate case, the matrix
\rho
reduces to
\int h
which corresponds to the average number of offspring "produced" by a point when the Hawkes process is viewed as a Poisson cluster process, see
[
32
]
. Moreover, there is a causal interpretation of
\rho
: one can say that the process
N^{l}
does not "Granger-cause"
N^{k}
if
\rho_{l,k}=0
, see
[
23
]
. It is notably worthwhile to test the nullity of some coefficients
\rho_{l,k}
, as done for parametric models
[
7
,
40
]
and in for a nonparametric model in
[
18
]
(in the univariate case). However the nonparametric result in
[
18
]
requires to know the background rate
\nu
and is specific to the univariate case, where under the null hypothesis the process is a Poisson process. For a multivariate Hawkes process, the process is Poisson only if
\rho_{l,k}=0
for all
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
, having only some
\rho_{l,k}=0
is not sufficient to find back a Poisson process and proposing such a nonparametric testing procedure in the multivariate case is both of practical and theoretical interest. In
[
58
]
the authors estimate consistently
\delta_{l,k}=\mathds{1}_{\rho_{l,k}\neq 0}
but under unsatisfying assumptions on the prior. Beyond testing nullity, they are other general key questions. Which functionals can be estimated at a fast rate? What is the efficiency theory in these models? What is the limiting behavior of the marginal posterior distribution of a smooth functional of
f
? In this paper, we aim to fill these gaps, which are important both from a frequentist and Bayesian perspective.

1.2
Our contribution

We consider the same asymptotic framework as in
[
21
,
58
]
where a stationary Hawkes process
N
with
true
parameters
f^{0}
is observed over a time window of order
T
and
T\rightarrow+\infty
. We are interested in the semiparametric estimation of
smooth
functionals
\psi(f^{0})\in\mathbb{R}
of the infinite dimensional parameter
f^{0}
. We first derive a convolution theorem which states that for a regular estimator
\hat{\psi}_{T}
, the asymptotic distribution of
\sqrt{T}(\hat{\psi}_{T}-\psi(f^{0}))
can be written as the convolution between some probability distribution and a Gaussian distribution with optimal variance
V_{0}
, denoted
\mathcal{N}(0,V_{0})
. An estimator achieving the limit distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,V_{0})
is said to be efficient. This derivation is done following the standard semiparametric theory as presented in chapter 25 of
[
62
]
: a nonparametric LAN expansion is proved, giving rise to a LAN inner product that is used to define
V_{0}
. Parametric LAN expansion for point processes has been studied in
[
39
]
(see chapter 4 and the references within) where a central limit theorem tailored to this purpose is proved.

Secondly, based on the general approach of
[
14
]
and using the recent results of
[
21
,
58
]
, we study the semiparametric Bernstein-von Mises (BvM) property for nonparametric priors on
f
. This property says that, given a smooth functional
\psi
and an efficient estimator
\hat{\psi}_{T}
, the marginal posterior distribution of
\sqrt{T}(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T})
converges weakly, in probability, to a
\mathcal{N}(0,V_{0})
as
T\rightarrow+\infty
. In this way, by the convolution theorem, semiparametric Bayesian inference is efficient from a frequentist point of view.
In the context of priors on the

h_{l,k}
based on expansions on a basis, we obtain in Theorem
3.2
an approximation of the posterior
distribution of functional
\psi(f)
as a mixture of Gaussian distributions. To understand when
this mixture of Gaussian distributions corresponds to
a single Gaussian distribution and thus that the BvM property holds, we need to study more precisely the least favourable direction and show that it can be well approximated under the prior distribution. The difficulty here is that the least favourable direction is non explicit and depends on the Palm distribution of the process, see Lemma
2.4
. Nevertheless we show, under Holder smoothness assumptions on the functions
h^{0}_{l,k}
, that the least favourable direction is Holder smooth (see corollary
4.1
). This allows us to derive explicit sufficient conditions for the BvM to hold. Moreover, in order to derive the asymptotic distribution of the marginal posterior, we also need to obtain posterior contraction rates in
L_{2}
, which is of independent interest.

The semiparametric BvM property allows efficient and flexible estimation of smooth functionals of the parameters. In particular,
1-\alpha
Bayesian credible intervals for
\psi(f)
are then asymptotically
1-\alpha
frequentist confidence intervals. It also allows to study the consistency of Bayes tests for
\rho_{l,k}=0
. Moreover, since the least favourable direction is not explicit, constructing concrete confidence intervals with a frequentist procedure is not straightforward, whereas Bayesian procedures provide an automatic approach using the quantiles of the marginal posterior distribution.

Our work is part of a broader line of research aimed at extending the BvM theorem to more general settings. Over the last two decades, the BvM theorem for parametric models (see section 10 of
[
62
]
for a statement with minimal assumptions) has been generalized in various ways. In particular,
[
55
]
,
[
15
]
and
[
14
]
(upon which we build) investigated the BvM property for semiparametric models while nonparametric version of the BvM property was proposed in
[
13
]
. We also point out that the BvM phenomena for parametric models that are non regular and exhibit boundary effects was analyzed in
[
5
]
. More recently, the semiparametric and the nonparametric BvM property have been investigated in more complex models than those initially considered in the seminal papers
[
15
,
13
,
14
,
55
]
. The nonparametric version, have been notably studied for nonlinear inverse problems
[
47
,
46
]
and for multidimensional diffusions
[
45
]
. Semiparametric BvM has been studied, among others, in Hidden Markov Models (HMM)
[
44
]
, for multidimensional diffusions
[
29
]
, in a missing data model (with a causal inference equivalent formulation)
[
51
]
and for some linear inverse problems
[
41
]
. In our case, as mentioned earlier, one of the main difficulties for the Hawkes model lies in the study of the regularity of the least favourable direction. The latter is defined as the image of the usual
L_{2}
Riesz representor of the functional by the inverse of the so called
information operator
which is at first glance non explicit in this model. In Section
2.3
, we obtain an explicit formula for this operator using Palm calculus and then we prove in Section
4
that under a Hölder regularity assumption on
h_{l,k}^{0}
, the least favourable direction is (almost) as regular as the
L_{2}
Riesz representor. Similar issues concerning the information operator and the least favourable direction have also arisen recently in some PDEs and inverse problems models, see
[
29
,
43
,
46
]
.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we present the results on the semiparametric efficiency (the LAN expansion, the convolution theorem and the derivation of an explicit formula for the information operator). Section
3
is dedicated to Bayesian inference, we state a theorem for the
L_{2}
posterior contraction, then we present our main results on the BvM property and we illustrate them on histogram and wavelet based priors. The study of the regularity of the least favourable direction is presented in Section
4
. In Section
5
, we prove our main result on the BvM property (Theorem
3.2
) and the main result for the regularity of the last favorable direction (Lemma
4.2
) which are the more interesting and novel proofs. In the Appendix
A
, we briefly introduce Palm calculus giving the results we use in this paper. Appendices
B
,
C
and
E
contain the other proofs and technical lemmas and the Appendix
F
recalls a Lemma from
[
58
]
which is used repeatedly throughout this paper.

1.3
Setup and notations

The parameters of a Hawkes process are
f=(\nu,\mathbf{h})
with
\nu=(\nu_{1},...,\nu_{K})\in]0,+\infty[^{K}
and
\mathbf{h}
is the
K\times K
matrix whose
k
-th column is
\mathbf{h}_{k}=(h_{1,k},...,h_{K,k})
. The intensity of the
k
-th process depends only on
f_{k}=(\nu_{k},\mathbf{h}_{k})
. To avoid confusion, we will always denote in bold matrices or vectors of functions. Throughout this paper we assume that the interactions functions
h_{l,k}
are supported on a bounded interval
[0,A]
with
A
known. It is a common assumption (see
[
21
]
and
[
31
]
) and it implies that the Hawkes process has a finite memory, some renewal properties (see
[
58
]
and
[
17
]
) and its intensity is equal to

\lambda_{t}^{k}(f_{k})=\nu_{k}+\sum_{l=1}^{K}\int_{t-A}^{t-}h_{l,k}(t-u)dN_{u}^{l},\hskip 8.5359ptk\in[K].

(1.2)

By Theorem 7 of
[
11
]
, if the
K\times K
matrix
\rho
with entry
(l,k)
equal to
\rho_{l,k}=\int_{0}^{A}h_{l,k}(x)dx
has a spectral radius
r(\rho)
strictly smaller than
1
, then there exists a unique stationary distribution for the
multivariate Hawkes process
N
with stochastic intensity given by (
1.2
). Hence, we define the following sets of parameters

\displaystyle\mathcal{\bar{H}}=\Big\{\mathbf{h}=(h_{l,k})_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}};\hskip 2.84544ptsupp(h_{l,k})\subseteq[0,A],\hskip 2.84544pt\forall(l,k)\in[K]^{2}\Big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\Big\{\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{\bar{H}}:\hskip 2.84544pt\forall(l,k)\in[K]^{2},h_{l,k}\geq 0,\hskip 2.84544ptr(\rho)<1\Big\}.

The statistical model we consider is
\mathcal{P}=\big\{\mathbb{P}_{f},\hskip 2.84544ptf=(\nu,\mathbf{h})\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}\big\}
, which is identifiable. We assume that the true generating process of
N
,
\mathbb{P}_{0}
, is associated to a
true
parameter
f^{0}\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
, so that
\mathbb{P}_{0}=\mathbb{P}_{f^{0}}
;
L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
denotes the space of random variables square integrable with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
and an expectation under
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is denoted
\mathbb{E}_{0}
. We denote by
\mathbb{P}_{0}(.|\mathcal{G}_{0-})
the conditional distribution of
\mathbb{P}_{0}
given
\mathcal{G}_{0-}
.

We assume that we observe on an interval
[-A,T]
the stationary multivariate linear Hawkes process with parameters
f^{0}
. For some parameters
f
, the log-likelihood on
[0,T]
conditionally on
\mathcal{G}_{0-}
(or equivalently on
\sigma\big(N^{k}([s,0[),-A\leq s<0,k\in[K]\big)
) is given by

\displaystyle L_{T}\big(f\big)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\log\big(\lambda_{t}^{k}(f)\big)dN_{t}^{k}-\int_{0}^{T}\lambda_{t}^{k}(f)dt.

(1.3)

\mathbb{P}_{f}
is the distribution of a Hawkes process parameterized by
f
and we have

\displaystyle d\mathbb{P}_{f}(.|\mathcal{G}_{0^{-}})=e^{L_{T}(f)-L_{T}(f^{0})}d\mathbb{P}_{0}(.|\mathcal{G}_{0^{-}}).

Although we are interested in linear multivariate Hawkes processes, we will also consider ReLU nonlinear multivariate Hawkes processes for which the functions
(h_{l,k})_{l,k}
can take negative values and the intensity of the
k
-th process is given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{t}^{k}(f_{k})=\Big(\nu_{k}+\sum_{l=1}^{K}\int_{t-A}^{t-}h_{l,k}(t-u)dN_{u}^{l}\Big)_{+}\hskip 2.84544pt,

(1.4)

with still
\nu_{k}>0
. Note that if the functions
(h_{l,k})_{l,k}
are non negative, a ReLU nonlinear Hawkes process and a linear Hawkes process both parameterized by
f
have the same intensity. Linear Hawkes processes are thus particular cases of nonlinear ReLU Hawkes processes. We denote by
\rho_{+}
the matrix with entry
(l,k)
equal to
\int_{0}^{A}h_{l,k}^{+}(x)dx
and we set
\mathcal{H}_{R}=\{\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{\bar{H}}:\hskip 2.84544ptr(\rho_{+})<1\}
. By Theorem 7 of
[
11
]
, given
f\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}_{R}
, there exists a unique stationary distribution with intensity (
1.4
). Now, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{R}=\Big\{(\nu,\mathbf{h})\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}_{R}:\hskip 2.84544pt\underset{k\in[K]}{\min}(\nu_{k}-\underset{l\in[K]}{\max}\|h^{-}_{l,k}\|_{\infty})>0\Big\}.

By proposition 2.3 of
[
58
]
, the model
\mathcal{P}_{R}=\big\{\mathbb{P}_{f},f\in\mathcal{F}_{R}\big\}
is identifiable. The reason why we consider this larger ReLU set of parameters
\mathcal{F}_{R}
is that
f^{0}
is on the boundary of
]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
when the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are not bounded away from
0
whereas
f^{0}
is always in the
interior
of
\mathcal{F}_{R}
. Being on the boundary of the parameters set modifies the BvM property and to avoid this, we will consider priors supported on
\mathcal{F}_{R}
in Section
3
when the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are not assumed to be bounded away from
0
. For this reason, we will also give a convolution theorem at
\mathbb{P}_{0}
in the model
\mathcal{P}_{R}
at the end of Section
2
.

The convergences in distribution, in probability and almost sure are respectively indicated by
\overset{d}{\rightarrow}
,
\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\rightarrow}
and
\overset{a.s.}{\rightarrow}
.
\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{A},d)
is the covering number of a set
\mathcal{A}
by balls of radius
\varepsilon
in terms of metric
d
. The symbol
\lesssim
will denote an inequality up to a positive multiplicative constant that might be universal or depend only on the true parameter
f^{0}
, and
a\asymp b
means that
a\lesssim b\lesssim a
. For
p\in[1,+\infty]
,
(L_{p}([0,A]),\|.\|_{p})
is the usual Lebesgue normed space on
[0,A]
and we simply write
L_{p}=L_{p}([0,A])
. The product space
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}L_{p}
is denoted
L_{p}^{n}
(typically
n=K
or
n=K^{2}
). Any norm on the space
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{p}^{K^{2}}
induces a norm on
L_{p}^{K^{2}}
and on
L_{p}
and we keep the same notation for the induced norms. In particular, for
p=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\nu,\mathbf{h})\|_{p}=\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{K}|\nu_{k}|_{p}^{p}+\sum_{l=1}^{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\|h_{l,k}\|_{p}^{p}\Big)^{1/p}\hskip 5.69046pt,

as well as
\|(\nu,\mathbf{h})\|_{\infty}=\max(\hskip 2.84544pt\max\{|\nu_{k}|,k\in[K]\},\hskip 2.84544pt\max\{\|h_{l,k}\|_{\infty},(l,k)\in[K]^{2}\})
.

Finally, let
\mathbf{h}
and
\mathbf{g}
be two
K\times K
matrices of functions,
\mathbf{h}.\mathbf{g}
and
\mathbf{h}./\mathbf{g}
are the
K\times K
matrices whose entries
(l,k)
are the functions
h_{l,k}g_{l,k}
and
h_{l,k}/g_{l,k}
(provided that
g_{l,k}\neq 0
), respectively. Given
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the matrix with entry
(l,k)
equal to
\gamma(h_{l,k})
is denoted
\gamma(\mathbf{h})
.

2
Semiparametric efficiency

In this Section, we study the efficiency theory on the estimation of smooth functionals
\psi(f)
as presented in
[
62
]
for i.i.d. models and in
[
42
]
for non i.i.d. models. To do so, we need to first derive the LAN property. Then, using the convolution theorem, we find the optimal asymptotic Gaussian distribution for the class of regular estimators. Finally, we obtain, using Palm calculus, an explicit expression for the
information operator
which can be useful to study the optimal variance for regular estimators.

2.1
LAN expansion

We expand the log-likelihood at second order around
(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})
. Let
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}}
, here and after the quantity
\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
is given by (
1.2
), namely

\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})=\xi_{k}+\sum_{l=1}^{K}\int_{t-A}^{t-}g_{l,k}(t-u)dN_{u}^{l}.

We take a path
(\xi_{T},\mathbf{g}_{T})
directed by
(\xi,\mathbf{g})
and passing through
f^{0}=(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})
as
T\rightarrow+\infty
. To simplify the presentation in this paragraph, we consider that
\mathbf{g}
is such that for
T
large enough the linear path
\mathbf{h}^{0}+\mathbf{g}/\sqrt{T}
is in
\mathcal{H}
. We write
\log(1+x)=x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}+x^{2}R(x),\hskip 5.69046ptx>-1
, and simple algebra (see the proof of Lemma
2.2
) implies

\displaystyle L_{T}\Big(\nu^{0}+\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{T}},\mathbf{h}^{0}+\frac{\mathbf{g}}{\sqrt{T}}\Big)-L_{T}\big(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0}\big)=W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\frac{\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}}{2}+R_{T}\big(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{T}},\frac{\mathbf{g}}{\sqrt{T}}\big),

(2.1)

with

\displaystyle W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\big(dN_{t}^{k}-\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})dt\big),\hskip 2.84544pt\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg],

and the remainder
R_{T}\big(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{T}},\frac{\mathbf{g}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)
is given by (
B.4
) in Section
B.2
. Next, we set

\displaystyle L_{2,h^{0}_{l,k}}:=\Big\{g:[0,A]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 2.84544pt\int_{0}^{A}\frac{g^{2}(x)}{\nu_{0}^{k}+h^{0}_{l,k}(x)}dx<+\infty\Big\}\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046ptL_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}:=\bigotimes_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}L_{2,h^{0}_{l,k}}.

The canonical product norm on the space
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
is written
\|.\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}
,

\displaystyle\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\xi_{k}^{2}+\sum_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}\int_{0}^{A}\frac{g_{l,k}^{2}(x)}{\nu_{0}^{k}+h^{0}_{l,k}(x)}dx.

Let
L_{2,h^{0}_{l,k}}^{+}:=\big\{g\in L_{2,h^{0}_{l,k}},g(x)\geq 0\hskip 2.84544pt\text{when }\hskip 2.84544pth^{0}_{l,k}(x)=0\big\}
and
L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+}:=\bigotimes_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}L_{2,h^{0}_{l,k}}^{+}
. The latter set is a convex cone, its linear span is
L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
and when the functions
h^{0}_{l,k}
are bounded away from
0
,
L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+}=L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
. Given the first order term
W_{T}
, we define the operator
\mathcal{S}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle:\hskip 2.84544pt\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}\xrightarrow{\hskip 14.22636pt}\mathcal{S}\big(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}\big)

(2.2)

\displaystyle\big(\xi,\mathbf{g}\big)\xrightarrow{\hskip 14.22636pt}\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})\big(\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}-dt\big)\bigg)_{k\in[K]}.

In the semiparametric terminology,
\mathcal{S}
is a score operator that maps a perturbation
(\xi,\mathbf{g})
to a tangent vector
\mathcal{S}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
(note that
\mathbb{E}_{0}[\mathcal{S}(\xi,\mathbf{g})]=0)
, the range of this operator is the tangent set. Furthermore, this operator defines a bilinear map over
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
by

\displaystyle\langle(\xi,\mathbf{g}),(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})\rangle_{L}:=\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\mathcal{S}(\xi,\mathbf{g})^{T}\mathcal{S}(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})\Big]=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\xi_{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{g}_{k}^{\prime})}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg].

\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}=\langle(\xi,\mathbf{g}),(\xi,\mathbf{g})\rangle_{L}
, we then show in the following lemma that
\langle\cdot{,}\cdot\rangle_{L}
is an inner product, we endow the space
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
with this LAN inner product. It plays a major role in semiparametric efficiency, see again chapter 25 of
[
62
]
and
[
42
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

The bilinear map
\langle\cdot{,}\cdot\rangle_{L}
is an inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
and induces the LAN norm
\|.\|_{L}
. In addition,
\|.\|_{L}
is equivalent to the canonical norm
\|.\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}
over
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
. Consequently,
\mathcal{S}
is a bounded, bijective, linear operator between the Hilbert spaces
\big(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}},\|.\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}\big)
and
\big(\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}),\|.\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0})}\big)
.

We can now state the result concerning the LAN expansion. Here the LAN property is in the sense of definition 2.1 of
[
42
]
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})
be in
]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
. The model
\mathcal{P}
verifies the LAN property at
\mathbb{P}_{0}
with respect to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+})
.

Lemmas
2.1
and
2.2
are proved in Section
B.1
and Section
B.2
respectively . Lemma
2.1
shows that the perturbations must belong to
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+}
and since
\mathcal{S}
is linear,
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+})
is the maximal tangent set (see chapter 25 of
[
62
]
for further details). In the proof of Lemma
2.2
, we take a nonlinear path, instead of the linear one used in (
2.1
), given by

\displaystyle(\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}}:=\bigg(\big(\nu_{k}^{0}+\frac{\xi_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)_{k\in[K]}\hskip 1.42271pt,\hskip 1.42271pt\Big(\Big(h_{l,k}^{0}+\frac{1}{a_{T}}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}g_{l,k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\Big)_{+}\Big)_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}\bigg)\hskip 2.84544pt,

(2.3)

with
(a_{T})_{T}
a positive sequence such that
a_{T}\rightarrow+\infty
and
a_{T}/\sqrt{T}\rightarrow 0
as
T
goes to
+\infty
. As
\arctan(x)\sim x
in
0
, this nonlinear path is asymptotically equivalent to the linear path of (
2.1
) and one can check that
the perturbed parameters defined by (
2.3
) are in
]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
for
T
large enough, since
\arctan
is bounded.

2.2
Convolution theorem

Using the LAN property, we can now obtain the convolution theorem for smooth functionals. For simplicity, we restrict to the case of real-valued functionals but the results presented in this paper can be directly extended to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued functionals,
d\geq 2
. Similarly to
[
62
]
and
[
42
]
, we consider that the functional
\Psi:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is differentiable at
\mathbb{P}_{0}
, i.e. there exists
\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}=(\xi^{0}_{L},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
such that for all
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\bigg(\Psi\big(\mathbb{P}_{(\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}}}\big)-\Psi\big(\mathbb{P}_{0}\big)\bigg)\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{}\langle(\xi,\mathbf{g}),\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\rangle_{L}\hskip 2.84544pt,

(2.4)

so that
\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}
is the least favourable direction. Since the model
\mathcal{P}
is identifiable,
\Psi(\mathbb{P}_{f})=\psi(f)
and from now on we work directly with
\psi(f)
. The LAN norm is equivalent to the norm
\|.\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}
(Lemma
2.1
) so (
2.4
) is equivalent to the existence of an element
\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{2}=(\xi^{0}_{2},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
such that for all
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\bigg(\psi\big((\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}}\big)-\psi(f^{0})\bigg)\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{}\langle(\xi,\mathbf{g}),\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{2}\rangle_{2}\hskip 2.84544pt,

(2.5)

and, denoting
\mathcal{S}^{*}
the adjoint operator of
\mathcal{S}
and letting
\Gamma:=\mathcal{S}^{*}\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{2}=\Gamma(\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}).

(2.6)

\Gamma
is called the
information operator
, see section 25.5 of
[
62
]
. For instance, the functionals

\displaystyle\psi^{b}_{k}(\nu,\mathbf{h})=\nu_{k};\hskip 5.69046pt\psi_{l,k}^{2}(\nu,\mathbf{h})=\int_{0}^{A}h_{l,k}^{2}(x)dx\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046pt\psi_{l,k}^{a}(\nu,\mathbf{h})=\int_{0}^{A}a(x)h_{l,k}(x)dx\hskip 2.84544pt,

(2.7)

with
a\in L_{2}
, verify (
2.5
) (and (
2.4
)) with
\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{2}
respectively given by

(e_{k},0)
,
(0,2h^{0}_{l,k}E_{l,k})
and
(0,aE_{l,k})
where
E_{l,k}
is the
K\times K
matrix that contains functions all equal to
0
except the one at entry
(l,k)
equal to the identity and
e_{k}
is the vector of length
K
with all element equal to
0
except the
k
-th one equal to
1
.

The LAN property and differentiability are the two assumptions of the convolution theorem (theorem 2.4 in
[
42
]
) for regular estimators (see again
[
42
]
or
[
62
]
for a definition).

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
f^{0}\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
. Consider a functional
\psi:]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
differentiable at
f^{0}
in the sense of (
2.5
) with least favourable direction
\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}
. The asymptotic distribution of every regular estimator of
\psi(f^{0})
can be written as the convolution between
\mathcal{N}\big(0,\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\|_{L}^{2}\big)
and some other probability distribution.

Note that we have chosen a path asymptotically equivalent to the additive path
\mathbf{h}^{0}+\mathbf{g}/\sqrt{T}
instead of a path equivalent to the multiplicative path
\mathbf{h}^{0}(1+\mathbf{g}/\sqrt{T})
so that the functions
h_{l,k}^{0}
can be perturbed on the sets
\{x:h_{l,k}^{0}(x)=0\}
. Note also that Theorem 12 of
[
42
]
is written for linear tangent spaces. When the functions
h^{0}_{l,k}
are not all bounded away from
0
, the tangent set
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+})
is not a linear space but a convex cone and the convolution theorem remains valid in this more general case, see
[
61
]
. The convolution theorem implies that a regular estimator is asymptotically
efficient if its limit distribution is
\mathcal{N}\big(0,\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\|_{L}^{2}\big)
. Furthermore, by proposition 5 of
[
42
]
, a regular estimator
\hat{\psi}_{T}
is efficient if and only if

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}_{T}=\psi(f^{0})+\frac{W_{T}(\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L})}{\sqrt{T}}+o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\big).

(2.8)

We point out that in some models with non open parameter set, it is possible to modify an efficient estimator by leveraging the property that defines the boundary of the parameter set and obtaining a new estimator, regular in the interior and with "better performance" on the boundary. For this reason, the notion of regular estimators and the convolution theorem can be seen as less relevant when the true parameters are on the boundary of the parameters set, see
[
61
]
for instance the parametric example 2.15. In our model
\mathcal{P}
, if the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are not bounded away from
0
, then
\mathbf{h}^{0}
is on the boundary of
\mathcal{H}
.

In the following Section
3
, although
f^{0}\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
, we will consider priors supported on a somehow larger set (compared to
]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
)
\mathcal{F}_{R}^{1}\subset\mathcal{F}_{R}
when it is not assumed that the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are bounded away from
0
. This is done to avoid more serious boundary effects for the BvM property (see the discussion just after Theorem
3.2
). We thus also derive the semiparametric efficiency at
\mathbb{P}_{0}
in the model
\mathcal{P}_{R}
. In this model, when
\mathbf{h}^{0}\geq 0
we consider perturbations
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
and we use the path

\displaystyle(\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}_{R}}:=\bigg(\big(\nu_{k}^{0}+\frac{\xi_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)_{k\in[K]}\hskip 1.42271pt,\hskip 1.42271pt\Big(h_{l,k}^{0}+\frac{1}{a_{T}}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}g_{l,k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\Big)_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}\bigg).

(2.9)

One can check that for
T
large enough
(\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}_{R}}\in\mathcal{F}_{R}
. Then, we have the following lemma on the LAN expansion and the convolution theorem at
f^{0}
in the model
\mathcal{P}_{R}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})
be in
]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
. The model
\mathcal{P}_{R}
verifies the LAN property at
\mathbb{P}_{0}
with respect to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}})
. Moreover, consider a functional
\psi:\mathcal{F}_{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
differentiable at
\mathbb{P}_{0}
in the sense that there exists
\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}=(\xi^{0}_{L},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
such that for all
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\bigg(\psi\big((\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}_{R}}\big)-\psi(f^{0})\bigg)\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{}\langle(\xi,\mathbf{g}),\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\rangle_{L}.

Then, the asymptotic distribution of every regular estimator of
\psi(f^{0})
can be written as the convolution between
\mathcal{N}(0,\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\|_{L}^{2})
and some other probability distribution.

This result is proved in Section
B.2
, with similar arguments as in model
\mathcal{P}
.

From now on , we assume that
\mathbf{h}^{0}
belongs to
L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
, with this assumption the space of perturbations
L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
is equal to
L_{2}^{K^{2}}
and the norms
\|.\|_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
and
\|.\|_{2}
are equivalent.

2.3
Explicit formula of the information operator

In this Section, we show how Palm calculus allows to obtain an explicit expression for the information operator
\Gamma
(Lemma
2.4
). In particular, this implies that the least favorable direction satisfies the fixed-point equation (
2.13
). This is of interest in order to study the optimal variance for regular estimators
\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\|_{L}^{2}
. This will also serve as our starting point in Section
4
, where we study the regularity of the least favorable direction to derive explicit conditions for the BvM property. In the Appendix
A
, we briefly introduce Palm calculus and we state the useful results that we use, giving each time references for the proofs and further details.

Let
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}}
and set
(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})=\Gamma(\xi,\mathbf{g})=\mathcal{S}^{*}\mathcal{S}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
. By definition of the LAN norm, we have for all
(\bar{\xi},\bar{\mathbf{g}})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}}
,
\langle(\bar{\xi},\bar{\mathbf{g}}),(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})\rangle_{2}=\langle(\bar{\xi},\bar{\mathbf{g}})(\xi,\mathbf{g})\rangle_{L}
which can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bar{\xi}_{k}\xi^{\prime}_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{K}\langle\bar{g}_{j,k},g^{\prime}_{j,k}\rangle_{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\bar{\xi}_{k},\bar{\mathbf{g}}_{k})\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg].

Taking
\bar{\mathbf{g}}=0
, we find that for all
k\in[K]
,
\xi^{\prime}_{k}=\xi_{k}\mathbb{E}_{0}\big[\frac{1}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\big]+\mathbb{E}_{0}\big[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\big]
. Then, let
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
, take
\bar{\xi}=0
and
\bar{\mathbf{g}}
with all entry equal to
0
except the entry
(l,k)
equal to some function
\bar{g}_{l,k}\in L_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\bar{g}_{l,k},g^{\prime}_{l,k}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\xi_{k}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\int_{0}^{A}\bar{g}_{l,k}(A-u)\frac{dN_{u}^{l}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big]+\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\int_{0}^{A}\bar{g}_{l,k}(A-u)g_{l,k}(A-u)\frac{dN_{u}^{l}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\sum_{j=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\int_{0}^{A}\int_{0}^{A}\mathds{1}_{(u,l)\neq(s,j)}\bar{g}_{l,k}(A-u)g_{j,k}(A-s)\frac{dN_{s}^{j}dN_{u}^{l}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg].

(2.10)

Now, for
(l,k,j)\in[K]^{3}
, we define the function
p_{l,k}:[0,A]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle p_{l,k}(A-u):=\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(u,l)}\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big],

(2.11)

and the operator
\zeta_{l,j,k}:L_{2}\rightarrow L_{2}
by

\displaystyle\zeta_{l,j,k}(g)(A-u):=\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(u,l)}_{0}\bigg[\int_{0}^{A}\mathds{1}_{(s,j)\neq(u,l)}g(A-s)\frac{dN_{s}^{j}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg],

(2.12)

where
\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(u,l)}
is an expectation under the Palm distribution
\mathbb{P}^{(u,l)}_{0}
of
N
, see the Appendix
A
. Then, using (
A.1
), we can rewrite the right hand side of (
2.10
) with the functions
p_{l,k}
and
\zeta_{l,j,k}(g_{l,k})
:

\displaystyle\langle\bar{g}_{l,k},g^{\prime}_{l,k}\rangle_{2}=\langle\bar{g}_{l,k},\mu_{l}^{0}\xi_{k}p_{l,k}\rangle_{2}+\langle\bar{g}_{l,k},\mu^{0}_{l}g_{l,k}p_{l,k}\rangle_{2}+\langle\bar{g}_{l,k},\mu_{l}^{0}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\zeta_{l,j,k}(g_{j,k})\rangle_{2}.

As this holds for any
\bar{g}_{l,k}\in L_{2}
, we deduce that
g^{\prime}_{l,k}=\mu_{l}^{0}\xi_{k}p_{l,k}+\mu_{l}^{0}g_{l,k}p_{l,k}+\mu_{l}^{0}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\zeta_{l,j,k}(g_{j,k})
. We have thus proved the following lemma.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}}
and set
(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})=\Gamma(\xi,\mathbf{g})
, for all
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
:

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}_{k}=\xi_{k}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big]+\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{\lambda_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big]\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 5.69046ptg^{\prime}_{l,k}=\mu_{l}^{0}\xi_{k}p_{l,k}+\mu_{l}^{0}g_{l,k}p_{l,k}+\mu_{l}^{0}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\zeta_{l,j,k}(g_{j,k}),

Conversely,
(\xi,\mathbf{g})
verifies the following fixed point equation:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\xi_{k}=\mathbb{E}_{0}\big[\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})^{-1}\big]^{-1}\bigg(\xi^{\prime}_{k}-\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big]\bigg)\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 5.69046pt\forall k\in[K]\hskip 2.84544pt,

(2.13)

\displaystyle g_{l,k}=\frac{g^{\prime}_{l,k}-\mu^{0}_{l}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\zeta_{l,j,k}(g_{j,k})}{\mu^{0}_{l}p_{l,k}}-\xi_{k}\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 5.69046pt\forall(l,k)\in[K]^{2}.

Note that the right hand side of (
2.13
) is well defined because for each
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
, the function
p_{l,k}
is lower bounded by some positive constant
c_{l,k}
(see Lemma
D.2
). In particular,
(\xi^{0}_{L},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L})
verifies this fixed point equation with
(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})=(\xi^{0}_{2},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2})
.

In the following section, we apply the above results to Bayesian semiparametric estimation of
\psi(f^{0})
and we study the BvM property in this context.

3
Main results for Bayesian inference

We recall that we observe on an interval
[-A,T]
a stationary and linear Hawkes process with parameters
f^{0}
. As in
[
21
]
and
[
58
]
, we assume for the inference the slightly stronger condition
|\hskip-1.13791pt\|\rho^{0}|\hskip-1.13791pt\|_{1}<1
with
|\hskip-1.13791pt\|.|\hskip-1.13791pt\|_{1}
the matrix operator norm related to the the norm
\|.\|_{1}
. We consider a Bayesian nonparametric approach and we put a prior
\Pi
on
f\in\mathcal{F}_{R}^{1}:=\mathcal{F}_{R}\cap\{f:\max_{l,k}(\rho_{+})_{l,k}<1\}
. The prior is thus allowed to put some mass on parameters
f=(\nu,\mathbf{h})
with some
h_{l,k}
taking negative values; as in the previous section,
\mathbb{P}_{f}
is then the distribution of a ReLU nonlinear Hawkes process. As in
[
21
]
, the posterior distribution is given by:

\displaystyle\Pi(B|N)=\frac{\int_{B}e^{L_{T}(f)}d\Pi(f)}{\int_{\mathcal{F}_{R}^{1}}e^{L_{T}(f)}d\Pi(f)}\hskip 2.84544pt,

with
L_{T}(f)
the log-likelihood (
1.3
). We study, under
\mathbb{P}_{0}
, the behavior of the posterior distribution when
T\rightarrow+\infty
. More specifically, we are interested in the marginal posterior distribution of
\sqrt{T}(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T})
, that we denote
\mathcal{L}^{\Pi}\big(\sqrt{T}(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T})|N\big)
, with
\tilde{\psi}_{T}
satisfying (
2.8
). Let
d_{BL}
be the bounded Lipschitz metric on the space of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
, we say that we have the (semiparametric) Bernstein-von Mises (BvM) property when

\displaystyle d_{BL}\Big(\mathcal{L}^{\Pi}\big(\sqrt{T}(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T})|N\big),\mathcal{N}(0,\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\|_{L}^{2}\big)\Big)\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{\mathbb{P}_{0}}0.

(3.1)

A first step to prove the BvM property (
3.1
) is to obtain posterior contraction on
f^{0}
with respect to some metric
d
and rate
\epsilon_{T}\rightarrow 0
:
\Pi(f:d(f,f^{0})\leq\epsilon_{T}|N)\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{0}}1
as
T\rightarrow+\infty
. Posterior contraction in
L_{1}
norm has been studied by
[
21
,
56
,
58
]
. To prove the BvM property, we need a similar result but with respect to the
L_{2}
norm.

We first present the family of prior models we consider. Then, we give assumptions from which we derive a
L_{2}
posterior contraction rate, which have an interest on its own. Next, with some additional assumptions, we state our most important results, those on the BvM property. Finally, these results are illustrated on specific prior models.

3.1
Prior model

We choose a prior distribution
\Pi
on the parameters
f=(\nu,\mathbf{h})
of the form
\Pi\propto(\Pi_{\nu}\otimes\Pi_{\mathbf{h}})\mathds{1}_{(\nu,\mathbf{h})\in\mathcal{F}_{R}^{1}}
.
\Pi_{\nu}
is a probability distribution on
]0,+\infty[^{K}
with a positive and continuous density and such that
\Pi_{\nu}(\|\nu\|_{\infty}\geq x)\lesssim x^{-a}
for some
a>1
and positive
x
large enough. For the functional part, the prior is based on finite random series, these models are widely used to obtain adaptive priors, see for instance Section 10.4 of
[
27
]
. For all integer
J\geq 1
, consider a family of linearly independent functions
B_{J}=(b_{1}^{J},....b_{J}^{J})\in L_{\infty}^{J}
, in
L_{2}
. The prior
\Pi_{\mathbf{h}}
is built by choosing first a random
J\geq 1
from
\Pi_{J}
, then drawing random coefficients
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta_{l,k})_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}
with
\theta_{l,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}
from
\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}|J}
, and finally setting

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{l,k}=\theta_{l,k}^{T}B_{J}=\sum_{i=1}^{J}\theta_{l,k}^{i}b_{i}^{J}\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 8.5359pt\forall(l,k)\in[K]^{2}\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 5.69046pt\mathbf{h}=\varphi(\tilde{\mathbf{h}})=\big(\varphi(\tilde{h}_{l,k})\big)_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}\hskip 2.84544pt,

where
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a given function, non-decreasing and
L
-Lipschitz for some
L>0
, typically used to ensure that
h_{l,k}\geq 0
.
\Pi_{\mathbf{h}}
is thus induced by the prior
\Pi_{J,\boldsymbol{\theta}}
on
(J,\boldsymbol{\theta})
which is defined hierarchically:
d\Pi_{J,\boldsymbol{\theta}}=d\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}|J}d\Pi_{J}
. To write shortly the
K^{2}
equalities
\tilde{h}_{l,k}=\theta_{l,k}^{T}B_{J}
, we will use the notation
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}B_{J}
. For the sake of simplicity, we consider the case where
J_{l,k}=J
for all
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
. Our results extend to the more general case easily.

We set
\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(j):=Span(b_{1}^{j},...,b_{j}^{j})
and we assume that there exists
R\geq 2
such that
\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(j)\subseteq\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(Rj)
for all
j\geq 1
, this is weaker than assuming that
(\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(j))_{j}
is increasing. We also impose that for all integer
j\geq 1
and all
\tilde{h}_{\theta}=\theta^{T}B_{j}\in\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(j)
,

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{2}\asymp\gamma(j)\|\tilde{h}_{\theta}\|_{2}\hskip 2.84544pt,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{h}_{\theta}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{j}\|\tilde{h}_{\theta}\|_{2}\hskip 2.84544pt,

(3.3)

for some positive and monotone sequence
\gamma
such that
\gamma
and
1/\gamma
have at most polynomial growth. Note that if
(b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is an orthornormal basis of
L_{2}
and
B_{j}=(b_{i})_{i\leq j}
(truncated orthonormal family), then
(\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(j))_{j}
is increasing, (
3.2
) is verified with
\gamma(j)=1
and, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, (
3.3
) is verified if
\|\sum_{i=1}^{j}b_{i}^{2}\|_{\infty}\lesssim j
. The latter is for instance true for Fourier basis or for a wavelet basis with bounded and compactly supported mother wavelet. Also, if
B_{j}
is a B-splines basis associated to the regular partition of
[0,A]
into
j
bins, then for all
j\geq 2
,
\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(j)\subset\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(2j)
and (
3.2
) and (
3.3
) are verified with
\gamma(j)=\sqrt{j}
(see lemma E.6 of
[
27
]
).

Finally, we assume there exists some
c_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle\Pi_{J}(J=j)\asymp e^{-c_{1}j\log(j)}\hskip 2.84544pt,

(3.4)

and for
\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}|J}
, we assume that there exists
U:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that for any
j\geq 1
and
M>0
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}|j}(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\geq M)\lesssim je^{-U(M)}.

(3.5)

These two conditions are common for random series priors (see again Section 10.4 of
[
27
]
).

We introduce some notations. Given
(B_{j})_{j}
, we denote by
P^{j}_{2}
and
P^{j}_{L}
the orthogonal projections on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(j)^{K^{2}}
in terms of
L_{2}
and LAN inner products respectively. Note that
P^{j}_{2}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}):=P^{j}_{2}(0,\tilde{\mathbf{h}})
is the orthogonal projection of
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}
on
\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(j)^{K^{2}}
in terms of the
L_{2}
norm. When it exists, we denote
\varphi^{-1}(\mathbf{h}^{0})
by
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}^{0}
and
(\nu^{0},\tilde{\mathbf{h}}^{0})
by
\tilde{f}^{0}
.

3.2
Posterior contraction in
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
norms

We now give the assumptions and the results concerning the posterior contraction, first in
L_{1}
norm and then in
L_{2}
norm. We begin with an assumption on
\mathbf{h}^{0}
.

Assumption
(A).

We assume that for all
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
, there exists a bounded function
\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}
such that
h^{0}_{l,k}=\varphi(\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k})
.

Note that assumption
(A)
allows
\varphi(x)=x\mathds{1}_{x\geq c_{0}}
provided that for all
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
,
h^{0}_{l,k}\geq c_{0}
and in this case
\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}=h^{0}_{l,k}
. We also assume that
\mathbf{h}^{0}
verifies the following assumption.

Assumption
(P1).

Let
c_{2}>0
and
c_{3}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
16c_{1}c_{3}\leq c_{2}
with
c_{1}
as in (
3.4
). Set
c_{4}:=c_{2}+11
and
J_{0}:=4c_{4}/(c_{1}c_{3})\geq 1
. We assume that there exist an integer valued sequence
(\bar{J}_{T})_{T}
and a positive sequence
(\bar{\varepsilon}_{T})_{T}
with
\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}\rightarrow 0
,
\log(T)^{3}\lesssim T\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}^{2}
and
\log(\bar{J}_{T})\leq\log(T)
, such that for
T
large enough and for all
j\in[\bar{J}_{T},RJ_{0}\bar{J}_{T}]
:

\displaystyle\log\big(\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}|j}\big(\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{2}j}:\forall(l,k)\in[K]^{2},\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}\leq\theta_{l,k}^{T}B_{j}\leq\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}+\frac{\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}}{L}\big)\big)\geq-\frac{c_{2}}{2}\bar{J}_{T}\log(T),

(3.6)

and such that for some
M_{T}\rightarrow+\infty
with at most polynomial growth,

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{T}=c_{3}\lceil T\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}^{2}\log(T)\rceil\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359ptc_{4}T\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}^{2}\log(T)^{2}\leq U(M_{T}).

(3.7)

This assumption is a specialization of the prior mass condition in
[
58
]
to our random series prior. If
\varphi(x)=x
and the prior
\Pi_{\mathbf{h}}
puts all its mass on non negative functions, it is possible to use the two-sided neighborhood
h^{0}_{l,k}-\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}\leq\theta_{l,k}^{T}B_{j}\leq h^{0}_{l,k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}
as in
[
21
]
. We set

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{T}=\log(T)\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046ptJ_{T}=J_{0}\bar{J}_{T},

(3.8)

We can now state the first posterior contraction result in terms of the
L_{1}
norm.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
N
be a stationary, linear and multivariate Hawkes process with parameters
f^{0}=(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})
such that
|\hskip-1.13791pt\|\rho^{0}|\hskip-1.13791pt\|_{1}<1
and
\mathbf{h}^{0}\in L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
. Let
\Pi
be a prior distribution on
\mathcal{F}_{R}^{1}
constructed as in section
3.1
and verifying the assumptions therein. Assume
(P1)
and if
\varphi
is not the identity function, assume also
(A)
. Then, for some
C_{1}>0

\displaystyle\Pi\big(\|f-f^{0}\|_{1}\geq C_{1}\varepsilon_{T}\big|N\big)\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{\mathbb{P}_{0}}0.

This proposition is a direct consequence of proposition 3.5 of
[
58
]
(case 1) for random series priors, we prove it in Section
C.1
. Note that assumption (
3.3
) is not used in this proof.

Next, to derive the
L_{2}
posterior contraction rate when
\varphi
is not the identity function, we need an other assumption on the sup-norm of the
h_{l,k}
drawn from the prior. For this assumption, we first define for
C_{1}>0
introduced in Proposition
3.1
, the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}(j):=\bigg\{f=(\nu,\mathbf{h})=(\nu,\varphi(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}B_{j}))\in\mathcal{F}_{R}:\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq M_{T}\hskip 2.84544pt,\|f-f^{0}\|_{1}\leq C_{1}\varepsilon_{T}\bigg\}\hskip 2.84544pt,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}=\bigcup_{j\leq J_{T}}\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}(j).

The assumption is then the following.

Assumption
(P2).

When the link function
\varphi
is not the identity function, it is assumed that there exist
G>0
and a positive sequence
(r_{T})_{T}
such that for
T
large enough,

\displaystyle\Pi\big(\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}\cap\{\hskip 2.84544pt\|\mathbf{h}\|_{\infty}\geq r_{T}\}\big)\lesssim e^{-c_{4}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}\hskip 2.84544pt,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\Pi\big(\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}\cap\{\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}\leq\log(T)r_{T}\varepsilon_{T},\hskip 2.84544pt\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{\infty}\geq G\}\big)\lesssim e^{-c_{4}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}.

(3.10)

with
c_{4}
defined in
(P1)
.

The
L_{2}
posterior contraction rate is then bounded by
\log(T)\varepsilon_{T}
as shown below.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
N
be a stationary, linear and multivariate Hawkes process with parameters
f^{0}=(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})
such that
|\hskip-1.13791pt\|\rho^{0}|\hskip-1.13791pt\|_{1}<1
and
\mathbf{h}^{0}\in L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
. Let
\Pi
be a prior distribution on
\mathcal{F}_{R}^{1}
constructed as in section
3.1
and verifying the assumptions therein. Assume
(P1)
holds and let
\varepsilon_{T}
and
J_{T}
, defined in (
3.8
), be such that
\varepsilon_{T}\sqrt{J_{T}\log(T)}\rightarrow 0
, and when
\varphi
is not the identity function, assume also
(A)
and
(P2)
. Then,

\displaystyle\Pi\big(f:\|f-f^{0}\|_{2}\geq\log(T)\varepsilon_{T}|N\big)\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{\mathbb{P}_{0}}0.

The proof of Theorem
3.1
is provided in Section
C.1
. When
\varphi
is the identity function, the assumptions in Theorem
3.1
are similar to those used in
[
21
]
and
[
58
]
for the weaker
L_{1}
-norm with the additional condition
\varepsilon_{T}\sqrt{J_{T}\log(T)}\rightarrow 0
. We will see in Section
3.4
, that under
\beta
-Hölder regularity, the latter condition is equivalent to
\beta>1/2

Let us briefly comment on assumption
(P2)
which is required when
\varphi
is not the identity function. First, with the preceding
L_{1}
posterior result we have
\Pi(\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}|N)\rightarrow 1
under
\mathbb{P}_{0}
, and for this reason the assumption only concerns
f\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}
. Secondly, this assumption is used for the "
L_{2}
" tests (with type 1 and 2 errors that decrease exponentially) needed for the
L_{2}
posterior contraction, see Section
C.2
. More precisely, the study of the
L_{2}
tests involves a lower bound on ratios of the type
\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}/\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{\infty}
and
(P2)
allows to deal with these ratios. Note that there is a trade off between (
3.9
) and (
3.10
): the smaller
r_{T}
, the easier it is to verify (
3.10
), but conversely, the harder it is to verify (
3.9
). In Lemma
C.10
, for priors constructed on a wavelet basis in Section
3.4
, we verify
(P2)
for a certain class of link functions
\varphi
.

3.3
The BvM property

The BvM property we derive rely on the previous
L_{2}
posterior but, when
\varphi
is not the identity function, we also need that the posterior on
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}
concentrates around
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}^{0}
at a rate of order
\varepsilon_{T}
up to
\log(T)
terms. For this, we strengthen assumption
(A)
. To do so, we denote by
I_{0}
the convex hull of the set
\big(\cup_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}h_{l,k}^{0}([0,A])\big)
and for
\epsilon>0
,
I_{0}(\epsilon)
denotes the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
I_{0}
.

Assumption
(A’).

We assume that for some
\epsilon>0
, the inverse of the link function
\varphi^{-1}
is well defined on
I_{0}(\epsilon)
and that
\varphi
is three times continuously differentiable on
\varphi^{-1}(I_{0}(\epsilon))
with a positive first derivative. Moreover, we assume that

\displaystyle\Pi\big(\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}\cap\{\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}\leq\log(T)\varepsilon_{T};\exists(l,k)\in[K]^{2},Range(h_{l,k})\not\subseteq I_{0}(\epsilon)\}\big)\lesssim e^{-c_{4}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}.

(3.11)

This assumption is made so that if
\varphi
is not the identity function, we can "linearize" it around the
\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}
by controlling the remainder terms. Note that for the function
\varphi(x)=x\mathds{1}_{x\geq c_{0}}
to verify assumption
(A’)
, it is necessary that the functions
h^{0}_{l,k}
are bounded away from
c_{0}
. As
(P2)
, in Lemma
C.10
, for priors constructed on a wavelet basis in Section
3.4
, we verify
(A’)
for a certain class of link functions
\varphi
. From now on, for
f=(\nu,\mathbf{h})=(\nu,\varphi(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}))
, we denote
(\nu,\tilde{\mathbf{h}})
by
\tilde{f}
. Let
\mathcal{J}_{T}\subseteq\{1,...,J_{T}\}
be such that
\Pi_{J}(\mathcal{J}_{T}|N)\rightarrow 1
under
\mathbb{P}_{0}
and let
M>0
. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)=\big\{f\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}(j):\|\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{0}\|_{2}\leq M\log(T)\varepsilon_{T}\big\}\hskip 5.69046pt,\hskip 8.5359pt\mathcal{A}_{T}:=\bigcup_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}\mathcal{A}_{T}(j).

(3.12)

With assumption
(A’)
, we show in Lemma
C.6
that
\Pi(\mathcal{A}_{T}|N)\rightarrow 1
under
\mathbb{P}_{0}
.

Now, regarding the functional
\psi
, we recall that
\psi
satisfies (
2.4
) with least favourable direction
\tilde{\psi}_{L}^{0}=(\xi^{0}_{L},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L})
if and only if it satisfies (
2.5
) with Riesz representor
\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{2}=(\xi^{0}_{2},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2})
and that
\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{2}=\Gamma(\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L})
. For
f\in\mathcal{A}_{T}
, we write

\displaystyle\psi(f)=\psi(f^{0})+\langle f-f^{0},\tilde{\psi}_{2}^{0}\rangle_{2}+r(f,f^{0}).

(3.13)

When
\varphi
is not the identity and verifies Assumption
(A’)
, set
\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}:=(\varphi^{\prime}(\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}),(l,k)\in[K]^{2})
. Since these functions are bounded away from
0
and
+\infty
, the bilinear map
\langle(\xi,\mathbf{g}),(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})\rangle_{L,\varphi}:=\mathbb{E}_{0}\big[\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}(\xi,\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}.\mathbf{g})^{T}\mathcal{S}_{\varphi}(\xi^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}.\mathbf{g}^{\prime})\big]
is an inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}}
. Moreover, the induced norm
\|.\|_{L,\varphi}
is equivalent to
\|.\|_{2}
on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}}
and
(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}},\langle\cdot{,}\cdot\rangle_{L,\varphi})
is a Hilbert space. Note that if
\varphi
is the identity function, then
\bar{\varphi}^{0}_{l,k}=1
for all
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
and
\langle\cdot{,}\cdot\rangle_{L,\varphi}=\langle\cdot{,}\cdot\rangle_{L}
. Let
P^{j}_{L,\varphi}
be the orthogonal projection operator on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{\tilde{H}}(j)^{K^{2}}
in terms of inner product
\langle\cdot{,}\cdot\rangle_{L,\varphi}
and set

\displaystyle\tilde{f}^{0,j}=P_{L,\varphi}^{j}(\tilde{f}^{0})\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 5.69046pt\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}=(\xi^{0}_{L},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0})\hskip 8.5359pt\text{and}\hskip 8.5359pt\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}=(\xi^{0,j}_{L,\varphi},\mathbf{g}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi})=P^{j}_{L,\varphi}(\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}).

With these notations and definitions, we can state our general BvM theorem.

Theorem 3.2
.

Consider a stationary multivariate Hawkes process
N
observed on
[-A,T]
, with parameters
f^{0}=(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
such that
|\hskip-1.13791pt\|\rho^{0}|\hskip-1.13791pt\|_{1}<1
and
\mathbf{h}^{0}\in L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
. Let
\Pi
be a prior distribution on
f
defined as in (
3.1
) and that verifies
(P1)
and, in case
\varphi
is not the identity function,
(A’)
and
(P2)
. Assume also that for some
\delta>0

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\varepsilon_{T}^{2(1-\delta)}\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{}0\hskip 11.38092pt\text{and}\hskip 11.38092pt\displaystyle\log(T)^{7}\sqrt{J_{T}}\varepsilon_{T}\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{}0.

(3.14)

Let
\psi
be a functional that verifies (
2.5
) with
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}\in L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
,
\sup\{\|\mathbf{g}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}\|_{\infty},\hskip 2.84544ptj\geq 1\}<+\infty
,

\displaystyle\underset{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}{\max}\|\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}-P^{j}_{2}(\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0})\|_{2}\rightarrow 0,

(3.15)

and such that
r(f,f^{0})
in (
3.13
) verifies

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\underset{f\in\mathcal{A}_{T}}{\sup}|r(f,f^{0})|\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{}0.

(3.16)

Finally, for
u\in\mathbb{R}
and
f=(\nu,\varphi(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}))
, let
f_{u,j}=\big(\nu-u\xi^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}/\sqrt{T},\varphi(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}-u\mathbf{g}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}/\sqrt{T})\big)
. Assume that for all
u
in a neighborhood of
0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}\frac{\int_{\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)}e^{L_{T}(f_{u,j})}d\Pi_{f|j}(f)}{\int_{\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)}e^{L_{T}(f)}d\Pi_{f|j}(f)}\Pi_{J}(j|N)\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{\mathbb{P}_{0}}1.

(3.17)

Then,

\displaystyle d_{BL}\bigg(\mathcal{L}^{\Pi}\big(\sqrt{T}(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T}-\mathcal{B}_{J,T})\big|N\big),\mathcal{N}\big(0,\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\|_{L}^{2}\big)\bigg)\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{\mathbb{P}_{0}}0,

(3.18)

with
\mathcal{B}_{J,T}=-\langle\tilde{f}^{0}-\tilde{f}^{0,J},\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0,J}_{L,\varphi}\rangle_{L,\varphi}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}W_{T}\big((\tilde{\psi}^{0,J}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}).\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}\big)
.

This theorem is proved in Section
5.1
. Roughly speaking, (
3.18
) says that the marginal posterior distribution of
\sqrt{T}\big(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T}\big)
can be approximated by a mixture of Gaussian distributions as
T\rightarrow+\infty
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\Pi}\Big(\sqrt{T}\big(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T}\big)\Big|N\Big)\approx\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}\mathcal{N}\big(\sqrt{T}\mathcal{B}_{j,T},\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\|_{L}^{2})\Pi_{J}(j|N).

Let us comment the conditions of Theorem
3.2
. The condition
\sqrt{T}\varepsilon_{T}^{2(1-\delta)}\rightarrow 0
is standard in semiparametric statistics (see for instance
[
14
]
). The second condition in (
3.14
) is more technical and is used in Lemma
E.2
, but essentially boils down to the first part of (
3.14
). In Section
3.4
, we show that in case of
\beta
-Hölder regularity, (
3.14
) is equivalent to
\beta>1/2
.

The functional smoothness condition (
2.5
) is standard and (
3.16
) comes from
[
14
]
. Regarding the condition on
\|\mathbf{g}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}\|_{\infty}
, by Lemma
4.1
, since
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}\in L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
, we have
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}\in L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
. Nevertheless, this condition is not trivial because
\mathbf{g}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}
is a projection in terms of LAN norm. We will see in Section
3.4
that
\sqrt{j}\|\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}-P^{j}_{2}(\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0})\|_{2}=O(1)
as
j\rightarrow+\infty
implies this condition (see Lemma
C.8
). The latter implies in particular that condition (
3.15
) is verified. Besides, condition (
3.15
) is weak since the rate of convergence towards
0
can be arbitrarily slow. As
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}\in L_{2}^{K^{2}}
, when
B_{j}
is a truncated total family of
L_{2}
, this condition is verified as soon as
\min\{j,j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}\}\rightarrow+\infty
.

Note that in condition (
3.17
), when
\varphi
is the identity, contrary wise to Theorem 2.1 of
[
14
]
, the perturbation is not
\mathbf{h}-u\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}/\sqrt{T}
but
\mathbf{h}-u\mathbf{g}^{0,j}_{L}/\sqrt{T}
which makes it much easier to verify. A consequence is that the posterior may be biased since it is centered at
\hat{\psi}_{T}+\mathcal{B}_{J,T}
instead of
\hat{\psi}_{T}
. A similar strategy was used in
[
14
]
in the density model for random histograms priors.

One way to verify (
3.17
) is to perform for each
j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}
the change of variable
f\rightarrow f_{u,j}
at the numerator of the ratio in the
j
-th summand and then to show that
d\Pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{h}}|j}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}+u\mathbf{g}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi})/d\Pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{h}}|j}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}})
goes uniformly to
1
on
\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)
. This might not be true if the prior
\Pi_{h_{l,k}}
puts all its mass on non-negative functions while
h^{0}_{l,k}
is not bounded away from
0
. The behavior of the ratios in (
3.17
), and consequently the behavior of
\mathcal{L}^{\Pi}\big(\sqrt{T}(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T})|N\big)
, are not clear in this boundary case. This is an interesting problem, both theoretically and for applications since it is not always realistic in practice to assume that the functions
h^{0}_{l,k}
are bounded away from
0
. This problem has been addressed in
[
5
]
for a broad class of parametric statistical models. They find that when the parameter is on the boundary of the prior support, the limiting distribution is (under some assumptions) a truncated Gaussian. We believe that something similar happens for the conditional distribution of the whole parameter

f
at least in some specific cases. This is left for future work.

When the
h^{0}_{l,k}
are not assumed to bounded away from
0
, to circumvent this issue we can use a prior which puts some mass on functions taking negative values. This is in particular how we proceed in section
3.4
for the random histograms prior.

Remark 3.1
.

It is possible to replace (
3.2
) by
\gamma_{1}(j)\|\tilde{h}_{\theta}\|_{2}\lesssim\|\theta\|_{2}\lesssim\gamma_{2}(j)\|\tilde{h}_{\theta}\|_{2}
for some positive sequences
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
such that
\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1}
has the same properties as
\gamma
in (
3.2
) but then the second condition in (
3.14
) becomes
\log(T)^{7}\sqrt{J_{T}}\varepsilon_{T}\max\big(\frac{\gamma_{2}(J_{T})}{\gamma_{1}(J_{T})},\frac{\gamma_{1}(J_{T})}{\gamma_{2}(J_{T})}\big)=o(1)
(see the entropy computations after (
E.10
) in the proof of Lemma
E.2
). Similarly,
\sqrt{j}
can be replaced by some positive sequence
\eta(j)\rightarrow+\infty
(with at most polynomial growth) in (
3.3
), the ideas remain the same but many conditions have to be adapted.

Remark 3.2
.

Lemma
C.5
, provided in Section
C.3
, shows that under the assumption

\displaystyle\log(T)^{3}\underset{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}{\max}\|\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}-P^{j}_{2}(\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0})\|_{2}\rightarrow 0,

(3.19)

which is slightly stronger than (
3.15
), the bias term
\mathcal{B}_{J,T}
in (
3.18
) can be replaced by

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{J}:=-\langle\tilde{f}^{0}-\tilde{f}^{0,J},\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0,J}_{L,\varphi}\rangle_{L,\varphi}.

Moroever,
|\mathcal{B}_{J}|\lesssim\log(T)\varepsilon_{T}\|\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}-P^{J}_{2}(\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0})\|_{2}
.

To have the BvM property, we thus need
\sqrt{T}\mathcal{B}_{j}\xrightarrow{}0
uniformly in
j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}
and so we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 3.1
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.2
, if

\displaystyle\log(T)\sqrt{T}\varepsilon_{T}\underset{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}{\max}\|\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}-P^{j}_{2}(\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0})\|_{2}\rightarrow 0,

(3.20)

then the posterior distribution has the BvM property (
3.1
).

Under the assumptions of Corollary
3.1
, when
\varepsilon_{T}
is the optimal
L_{2}
rate (up to a logarithmic factor), the Bayesian procedure has the
plug-in
property as in
[
4
]
: the posterior distribution concentrates "optimally" on
f^{0}
and simultaneously estimates a large class of smooth functionals efficiently at rate
1/\sqrt{T}
.

Condition (
3.20
) is the key condition for the BvM to hold in our framework. It requires that the least favourable direction can be
well
approximated by functions
\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}B_{j}
for
j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}
. In
[
14
]
, in the density model and for specific functionals the authors prove that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}-P_{2}^{j}(\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L})\|_{2}\lesssim\|\mathbf{h}^{0}-P_{2}^{j}(\mathbf{h}^{0})\|_{2}\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 14.22636ptj\geq j_{0},

(3.21)

which implies that (
3.20
) holds as soon as
\log(T)^{2}\sqrt{T}\varepsilon_{T}^{2}=o(1)
. This is particularly interesting since it implies that BvM holds for hierarchical priors which lead to adaptive posterior contraction rates on
f
. For other functionals, they show that on the contrary (
3.21
) does not hold and the posterior may not verify BvM due to a bias term coming from (
3.20
).

Here the situation is more complex since
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
does not have an explicit expression (see section
4
) and it is unclear if (
3.21
) holds. A different proof strategy then consists in showing that if
\mathbf{h}^{0}
and
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}
belong to some functional class then so does
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
and that for such class (
3.20
) is verified
so that BvM holds. We show in corollary
4.1
that if the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
and
\mathbf{g}^{2}_{0}
are
\beta
-Hölder, then the functions
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
are also (almost)
\beta
-Hölder. Then under reasonable assumptions on the prior,
\varepsilon_{T}\asymp T^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)}(\log T)^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\geq 0
and to ensure that (
3.20
) holds and thus that the BvM property is verified, it is enough to have
\beta>1/2
as well as for some
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Pi(J\gtrsim T^{1/(2\beta+1)}(\log T)^{\alpha^{\prime}}|N)=o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)
. In particular we can either choose a priori
J
deterministically, i.e. as the Dirac mass

\Pi_{J}=\delta_{(T^{1/(2\beta+1)}J_{1})}
, which leads to a non adaptive posterior distribution or assume that the functions
h_{l,k}^{0}
verify the polish tail condition as in
[
57
]
.

We apply in the following section our results to some specific random series priors.

3.4
Application on specific prior models

We present two types of random series priors, one constructed on histogram bases and the other on truncated wavelet bases, that satisfy the assumptions of the BvM results of Section
3
. First, we recall some standard notations for Hölder and Besov spaces. Let
I
be a sub-interval of
\mathbb{R}
, possibly equal to
R
, for
\beta>0
we denote by
C^{\beta}(I)
the usual Hölder space of order
\beta
on
I
. When
\beta\notin\mathbb{N}
,
C^{\beta}(I)
is equal to the Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}(I)
and when
\beta\in\mathbb{N}
,
C^{\beta}(I)\subsetneq\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}(I)
. The spaces
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}(I)
are also called the Hölder-Zygmund spaces. Throughout this section, to simplify the presentation we consider that the following assumption on the true parameters
f^{0}
and on the functional holds.

Assumption
(S).

The true parameters
f^{0}\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
are such that
|\hskip-1.13791pt\|\rho^{0}|\hskip-1.13791pt\|_{1}<1
and the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
for some
\beta>1/2
. Moreover, the functional
\psi
verifies (
2.5
), can be expanded as in (
3.13
) with
r(f,f^{0})=O(\|f-f^{0}\|_{2}^{2})
and is such that the functions
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}
are also in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
.

The condition on the remainder of the functional is slightly more demanding than what is required in Theorem 3.2. For instance, the functionals
\psi^{b}_{k}
,
\psi^{2}_{l,k}
and
\psi^{a}_{l,k}
with
a\in C^{\beta}([0,A])
(all three defined in (
2.7
)) verify the functional conditions in
(S)
. By corollary
4.1
, when
\beta\in]1/2,1]
, the functions
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
are in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}([0,A])
under assumption
(S)
. When
\beta>1
, assuming the Conjecture
1
and under assumption
(S)
, the functions
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
are in
\mathcal{C}^{\beta^{\prime}}([0,A])
for any
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
by corollary
D.1
.

For ease of presentation, we specify the prior distribution of
\boldsymbol{\theta}|J
:
\Pi_{\boldsymbol{\theta}|J}=\otimes_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}\Pi_{\theta|J}
and
\Pi_{\theta|J}=\otimes_{1\leq j\leq J}\bar{\Pi}_{\theta,j}
for some distributions
(\bar{\Pi}_{\theta,j})_{j}
that have densities
(\pi_{\theta,j})_{j}
with respect to Lebesgue measure. Similarly, we take
\Pi_{\nu}=\otimes_{1\leq k\leq K}\bar{\Pi}_{\nu}
with
\bar{\Pi}_{\nu}
a probability distribution on
]0,+\infty[
with a density denoted
\pi_{\nu}
assumed to be positive and continuously differentiable on
]0,+\infty[
and such that
\bar{\Pi}_{\nu}([x;+\infty[)\lesssim x^{-a}
for
x
large enough.

3.4.1
Random histogram priors

We assume that
\beta\in]1/2,1]
. We take for
B_{j}
the histogram basis associated to the regular partition of
[0,A]
into
j
bins:
B_{i}^{j}(x)=\mathds{1}\{x\in[\frac{A(i-1)}{j},\frac{Ai}{j}[\}
for
1\leq i\leq j-1
and
B_{j}^{j}(x)=\mathds{1}\{x\in[\frac{A(j-1)}{j},A]\}
. This is a particular case of B-splines basis (as defined in appendix E of
[
27
]
) when
q=1
.
(B_{j})_{j}
verifies the conditions of Section
3.1
with
\gamma(j)=\sqrt{j}
. We then choose
\varphi(x)=x
and for all
j\geq 1
,
\pi_{\theta,j}=\pi_{\theta}
where
\pi_{\theta}
is supported on
[\kappa,+\infty[
, for some
\kappa\in[-\infty,0]
and is positive and continuously differentiable on
]\kappa,-\infty[
. For simplicity, we also consider that
\pi_{\theta}
has sub-exponential tails. We verify the assumptions of Theorem
3.2
. First, (
3.5
) is verified with
U(M)\asymp M
and the assumption
(P1)
is verified with in particular

\displaystyle\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}(\beta)\asymp T^{\frac{-\beta}{2\beta+1}}\log(T)^{\frac{\beta}{2\beta+1}}\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 8.5359pt\bar{J}_{T}(\beta)\asymp T^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\log(T)^{\frac{3\beta+1}{2\beta+1}}.

(3.22)

When
\beta>1/2
, (
3.14
) is verified and, under assumption
(S)
, (
3.16
) is also verified. Moreover, since the functions
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
are in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}([0,A])
, we have
\|\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}-P_{2}^{j}(\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L})\|_{2}\lesssim j^{-\beta^{\prime}}
with
\beta^{\prime}=\beta
when
\beta<1
and
\beta^{\prime}<1
when
\beta=1
. Thus, by Lemma
C.8
, the condition
\sup\{\|\mathbf{g}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}\|_{\infty},j\geq 1\}<+\infty
is verified and, letting
j_{T}:=\min\{j,j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}\}
, (
3.15
) is verified when
j_{T}\rightarrow+\infty
, whatever the rate. By Lemma
C.9
, if the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are bounded away from
\kappa
, then (
3.17
) is verified. Finally, condition (
3.20
) of Corollary
3.1
holds as soon as

\displaystyle\log(T)\sqrt{T}\varepsilon_{T}(\beta)j_{T}^{-\beta^{\prime}}\rightarrow 0,

(3.23)

We thus have proved the following corollary.

Corollary 3.2
.

Let
f^{0}
and
\psi
that verify assumption
(S)
with
\beta\in]1/2,1]
. Let
\Pi
be a prior constructed as above on the regular histogram bases with
\kappa
such that the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are bounded away from
\kappa
. If
j_{T}\rightarrow+\infty
, then (
3.18
) holds. If in addition condition (
3.23
) is satisfied, then the posterior distribution has the BvM property (
3.1
).

Examples of densities
\pi_{\theta}
are the shifted exponential, truncated Laplace and truncated Gaussian densities. In particular, if the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are bounded away from
0
, one can take
\kappa=0
and the standard exponential distribution is a possible choice. Otherwise, one has to take
\kappa<0
and the prior puts some mass on functions taking negative values, in other words it is a prior in the nonlinear ReLU model. A similar result holds for B-splines for any
\beta>1/2
.

3.4.2
Wavelet priors

Let
\beta>1/2
. Let
\big\{\Phi_{i,v},i\in\{-1,0\}\cup\mathbb{N},v\in\{0,...,\bar{v}_{i}-1\}\big\}
be a boundary-corrected Daubechies wavelet basis on
[0,A]
of regularity
S\in\mathbb{N}
with
S>\beta
(see section 4.3.5 of
[
28
]
). For all
i\geq-1
, we have
0\leq\bar{v}_{i}\leq C_{v}A2^{i}
for some
C_{v}>0
. Let
\bar{B}_{I}:=\big\{\Phi_{i,v},i\in\{-1,0,1,....,I\},v\in\{0,...,\bar{v}_{i}\}\big\}
(the truncated basis at
I
). To be in line with our conventions of Section
3.1
, we note that we can rewrite
\bar{B}_{I}
as a family
B_{J}=(b_{1}^{J},...b_{J}^{J})
with
J=c(I):=\sum_{i=-1}^{I}\bar{v}_{i}
. However, we work with the resolution level
I
instead of
J
as it is commonly done with wavelets. We thus put a prior on
I
which induces a prior on
J
. The sequences
(\bar{I}_{T}(\beta))_{T}
and
(I_{T}(\beta))_{T}
and the set
\mathcal{I}_{T}
are then defined analogously to
(\bar{J}_{T}(\beta))_{T}
,
(J_{T}(\beta))_{T}
and
\mathcal{J}_{T}
respectively. To simplify the computations, given
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{l,k},(l,k)\in[K]^{2})
with for each
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{l,k}=(\theta_{l,k}^{i},i\in\{-1,0,....,I\})
and for each
i\geq-1
,
\theta^{i}_{l,k}=(\theta^{i,v}_{l,k},v=0,...,\bar{v}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{\bar{v}_{i}}
(and thus
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{l,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{c(I)}
and
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{2}c(I)}
), we set

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\theta}^{i,T}_{l,k}\bar{B}_{I}^{i}:=\sum_{v=0}^{\bar{v}_{i}}\theta_{l,k}^{i,v}\psi_{i,v}\hskip 2.84544pt,\hskip 2.84544pt\boldsymbol{\theta}_{l,k}^{T}\bar{B}_{I}=\sum_{i=-1}^{I}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{i,T}_{l,k}\bar{B}_{I}^{i}\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046pt\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}\bar{B}_{I}=(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{l,k}^{T}\bar{B}_{I},(l,k)\in[K]^{2}).

(3.24)

These truncated bases verify the conditions of Section
3.1
with
\gamma=1
. Contrary to histograms, there is no obvious choice of the wavelet coefficients that ensures the non negativeness of the functions. We can either consider the ReLU nonlinear Hawkes model or choose a nonlinear
\varphi\geq 0
. We study both.

In the ReLU nonlinear model, we thus choose
\varphi(x)=x
. For the prior on the wavelets coefficients, we take for all
i\geq-1
and
v\in\{0,...,\bar{v}_{i}\}
,
\pi_{\theta,i,v}=\pi_{\theta}
where
\pi_{\theta}
is a density which is positive and continuously differentiable on
\mathbb{R}
with, for simplicity, sub-exponential tails (for example a Laplace or a Gaussian density). Then, similarly to what we did for random histograms, one can check, assuming Conjecture
1
when
\beta>1
and using standard approximation property of (boundary-corrected) wavelets (see again section 4.3.5 of
[
28
]
), that
(P1)
, (
3.14
) and (
3.16
) are verified with
\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}(\beta)
as in (
3.22
) and
\bar{I}_{T}(\beta)
such that
2^{\bar{I}_{T}(\beta)}\asymp T^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\log(T)^{\frac{3\beta+1}{2\beta+1}}
. Moreover, Lemma
C.8
allows again to verify that
\sup\{\|\mathbf{g}^{0,i}_{L,\varphi}\|_{\infty},i\geq-1\}<+\infty
, (
3.17
) is verified by Lemma
C.9
and, letting
i_{T}:=\min\{i,i\in\mathcal{I}_{T}\}
, (
3.15
) is verified when
i_{T}\rightarrow+\infty
. We obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
f^{0}
and
\psi
that verify assumption
(S)
with
\beta>1/2
. If
\beta>1
, assume in addition the Conjecture
1
. Let
\Pi
be a prior constructed as above on the truncated wavelet bases in the ReLU model. If
i_{T}\rightarrow+\infty
, then (
3.18
) holds. If in addition we have
\log(T)\sqrt{T}\varepsilon_{T}(\beta)2^{-i_{T}\beta^{\prime}}\rightarrow 0
for some
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
, then the posterior distribution has the BvM property (
3.1
).

Now, we present wavelets priors with nonlinear
\varphi\geq 0
and in order to verify assumption
(A’)
, we assume that the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are bounded away from
0
. We also assume that
\beta>\beta_{m}
for some
\beta_{m}\geq 1/2
defined in Corollary
3.4
. We choose
\varphi\geq 0
, globally non-decreasing and Lipschitz as well as infinitely differentiable with a positive first derivative on
\varphi^{-1}(I_{0}(\epsilon))
for some
\epsilon>0
that can be taken arbitrarily small. For example possible choices are
\varphi(x)=x_{+}
or
\varphi(x)=\log(1+e^{x})
(the
softplus
function). Then , recall that since the functions
\tilde{\mathbf{h}}^{0}
are in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}([0,A])
with
\beta>\beta_{m}
, for
i\geq 0
the order of magnitude of their wavelets coefficients is at most
2^{-i(\beta_{m}+1/2)}
. For the prior on the wavelets coefficients, we consider two cases. In case (i), we choose
\bar{\Pi}_{-1,r}=\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and for
i\geq 0
,
\bar{\Pi}_{i,r}=\mathcal{N}(0,2^{-i(2\beta_{m}+1)})
. In case (ii), we take
\bar{\Pi}_{-1,r}=\mathcal{U}([-C_{0},C_{0}])
(with
C_{0}>0
chosen large enough and that depends only on
\mathbf{h}^{0}
,
\varphi
and on the Daubechies wavelet basis) and for
i\geq 0
,
\bar{\Pi}_{i,r}=\mathcal{U}([-\log(T)^{2}2^{-i/2},\log(T)^{2}2^{-i/2}])
Then, as for the wavelet prior in the nonlinear ReLU model, we verify easily with the same
\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}(\beta)
and
\bar{I}_{T}(\beta)
the conditions required in theorem
3.2
when
\varphi
is the identity function. Moreover, we have to verify here the assumptions
(P2)
and
(A’)
since
\varphi
is not the identity function; we do it in Lemma
C.10
. We thus have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
f^{0}
and
\psi
that verify assumption
(S
) with
\beta>\beta_{m}
for some
\beta_{m}\geq 1/2
. If
\beta>1
, assume in addition the Conjecture
1
. Assume also that the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are bounded away from
0
. Let
\Pi
be a prior constructed as above on the truncated wavelet bases with
\varphi
that is infinitely differentiable and with a positive first derivative on
\varphi^{-1}(I_{0}(\epsilon))
for some
\epsilon>0
that can be taken arbitrarily small. Take
\beta_{m}=5/6
in case (i) and
\beta_{m}=1/2
in case (ii). If
i_{T}\rightarrow+\infty
, then (
3.18
) holds. If in addition we have
\log(T)\sqrt{T}\varepsilon_{T}(\beta)2^{-i_{T}\beta^{\prime}}\rightarrow 0
for some
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
, then the posterior distribution has the BvM property (
3.1
).

In case (i), the condition
\beta>\beta_{m}=5/6
comes from the verification of
(P2)
and
(A’)
(see Lemma
C.10
). In case (ii), it would be natural to take for i
\geq 0
,
\bar{\Pi}_{i,r}=\mathcal{U}([-C_{0}2^{-i},C_{0}2^{-i}])
to "match" the decrease of the wavelet coefficients (as we did in case (i) with the Gaussian distributions), however with these uniform distributions our proof of Lemma
C.9
fails and it is not clear whether the change of variable condition (
3.17
) is verified.

4
Regularity of the least favourable direction

In this section, we study the regularity of the least favourable direction
(\xi^{0}_{L},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L})
which is only known through the linear equation (
2.6
):
\Gamma(\xi^{0}_{L},\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L})=(\xi_{2}^{0},\mathbf{g}_{2}^{0})
where
\Gamma=\mathcal{S}^{*}\mathcal{S}
is the
information operator
. We use the notations
C^{\beta}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}
, recalled at start of section
3.4
, for Hölder and Besov (or Hölder-Zygmund) spaces respectively. We set
C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R}):=C^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})\cap L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty,b}(\mathbb{R}):=\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}\cap L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

First, by Lemma
2.1
and with standard properties of the adjoint operator, we know that the information operator
\Gamma
is a bounded, bijective, linear operator of
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}}
. By Banach-Schauder theorem, its inverse is also bounded. We recall also that Lemma
2.4
gives an explicit expression for the operator
\Gamma
involving the Palm distribution of the process and it implies that if
(\xi,\mathbf{g})=\Gamma^{-1}(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})
for some
(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}}
, then
(\xi,\mathbf{g})
verifies the fixed point equation (
2.13
). Starting from this, we study
\Gamma
as an operator on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}=\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}([0,A])^{K^{2}}
and for technical reasons we extend it on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}}
, we denote by
\Gamma^{e}
its extension. Next, using these results, we show that when the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
for some
\beta\in]0,1]
, the operator
(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}
maps
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times C^{\beta}([0,A])^{K^{2}}
to
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}([0,A])^{K^{2}}
(Lemma
4.2
). Hence, it shows that when the interaction functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
and the
L_{2}
Riesz representor
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}
are in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
for some
\beta\in]0,1]
, the functions
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
are in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}([0,A])
. An extension of this result for
\beta>1
is presented in Section
D.4
under a certain regularity assumption on the second order Palm distribution (see conjecture (
1
)). Palm calculus is briefly presented in the Appendix
A
where we state the results that we use, giving each time references for the proofs and further details.

To extend
\Gamma
on
