\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}}
, we first extend on
\mathbb{R}
the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
, the functions
(p_{l,k},(l,k)\in[K]^{2})
and the operators
(\zeta_{l,j,k},(l,j,k)\in[K]^{3})
(the last two being defined by (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) respectively). We consider bounded extensions
\mathbf{h}^{0,e}
on
\mathbb{R}
of the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
such that for each
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
,
h^{0,e}_{l,k}\geq-\nu_{k}^{0}/2
. For
t,u\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\displaystyle\lambda_{t}^{k,e}(f^{0}_{k},(u,l)):=\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})+h^{0,e}_{l,k}(t-u)\mathds{1}_{u\notin[t-A,t[}\geq\frac{\nu^{0}_{k}}{2}.

(4.1)

Then, recalling that
\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(u,l)}
is an expectation under the Palm distribution
\mathbb{P}^{(u,l)}_{0}
of
N
(see the Appendix
A
), we define the function
p_{l,k}^{e}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle p^{e}_{l,k}(A-u):=\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(u,l)}\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda_{A}^{k,e}(f^{0}_{k},(u,l))}\Big],

(4.2)

and for
x\in[0,A]
,
p_{l,k}(x)=p^{e}_{l,k}(x)
. The operator
\zeta^{e}_{l,j,k}:L_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is defined by

\displaystyle\zeta_{l,j,k}^{e}(g)(A-u):=\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(u,l)}_{0}\bigg[\int_{0}^{A}\mathds{1}_{(s,j)\neq(u,l)}g(A-s)\frac{dN_{s}^{j}}{\lambda_{A}^{k,e}(f^{0}_{k},(u,l))}\bigg],

(4.3)

and the restriction of
\zeta_{l,j,k}^{e}
to
L_{2}=L_{2}([0,A])
(so a linear operator of
L_{2}
which is well defined because for
g\in L_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
\zeta_{l,j,k}^{e}(g)
depends only on
g(x)
for
x\in[0,A]
) is equal to the operator
\zeta_{l,j,k}
. With these extensions and notations, we can now state the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1
.

The information operator
\Gamma
is a bounded, bijective linear operator of
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}=\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}([0,A])^{K^{2}}
and by Banach-Schauder theorem, its inverse is also a bounded operator on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
.

Moreover, let
\Gamma^{e}:\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L^{K^{2}}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L^{K^{2}}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be defined by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathbf{g})

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{k}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{1}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big]+\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big],\hskip 4.26773ptk\in[K]\\
\mu_{l}^{0}p^{e}_{l,k}g_{l,k}+\mu_{l}^{0}p^{e}_{l,k}\xi_{k}+\mu_{l}^{0}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\zeta^{e}_{l,j,k}(g_{j,k}),\hskip 4.26773pt(l,k)\in[K]^{2}\end{bmatrix}.

\Gamma^{e}
is equal to
\Gamma
on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}([0,A])
in the sense that
\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathbf{g})_{|[0,A]}=\Gamma(\xi,\mathbf{g}_{|[0,A]})
. In addition,
\Gamma^{e}
is a bounded, bijective linear operator of
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}(\mathbb{R})
with a bounded inverse.

Lemma
4.1
is proved in Section
D.1
. When
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}\in L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
, this lemma shows in particular that
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}\in L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}
too. Note that the equality
(\xi,\mathbf{g})=(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})
for bounded functions
\mathbf{g}^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{R}
can be written similarly to (
2.13
) as a fixed point equation on
\mathbb{R}
.

Now, with these results, we are ready to study the smoothness of
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
. We assume that the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
for some
\beta\in]0;1]
and we extend these functions on
\mathbb{R}
by functions
\mathbf{h}^{0,e}
that are in
C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
(this is possible by Whitney extension theorem). Moreover, we choose the functions
h^{0,e}_{l,k}
such that again
h^{0,e}_{l,k}\geq-\nu^{0}_{k}/2
. We want to show that
(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{B}_{\infty,\infty,b}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}}
which would imply the announced result:
\Gamma^{-1}\big(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times C^{\beta}([0,A])^{K^{2}}\big)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}([0,A])^{K^{2}}
. First, Lemma
D.2
and Lemma
D.3
show that the functions
(p^{e}_{l,k},(l,k)\in[K]^{2})
are in
C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
and bounded away from
0
, the proofs are based on some basic properties of the first order Palm distribution of the process recalled in Appendix
A
. Then, let
(\xi,\mathbf{g})=(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})
with
\mathbf{g}^{\prime}\in C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}}
, given the fixed point equation verified by
\mathbf{g}
, if we could show that
\zeta^{e}_{l,j,k}(g_{j,k})\in C^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})
whatever
(l,j,k)\in[K]^{3}
, it would prove the desired result. The difficulty here is that we do not have an explicit expression for
\mathbf{g}
in terms of
(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})
and also that what lies behind the operators
\zeta^{e}_{l,j,k}
is the second order Palm distribution of the Hawkes process (see (
D.4
)) which is much harder to study than the first order Palm distribution (see the Appendix
A
for an explanation on this terminology). To circumvent this difficulty, our strategy consists in proving that the functions in
(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}})
can be approximated at a "
\beta
-Hölder" rate in sup-norm by a convolution kernel adapted to Hölder smoothness. Then, by Littlewood–Paley characterization of Besov spaces on
\mathbb{R}
, we deduce the result. There is no analogous Littlewood–Paley characterization on
[0,A]
and this notably why we have extended to
\mathbb{R}
the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
and and the operator
\Gamma
. With these, we obtain the following Lemma
4.2
.

For the convolution kernel, let
\mathcal{K}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a bounded function, supported on
[-1,1]
, symmetric about
0
, with a bounded (weak) derivative on
\mathbb{R}
and such that
\int\mathcal{K}(x)dx=1
. For
n\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{K}_{n}(x)=n\mathcal{K}(nx)
and for
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{K}_{n}g(x)=\int\mathcal{K}_{n}(y)g(x-y)dy
. For
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}(\mathbb{R})
, let
\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}:=(\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{l,k},(l,k)\in[K]^{2})
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
for some
\beta\in]0,1]
, and let
(\xi,\mathbf{g})=(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})
for some functions
\mathbf{g}^{\prime}
that are in
C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
. Then, let
\mathcal{K}
be a kernel as above, we have
\|\mathbf{g}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}\|_{\infty}\leq C(\mathbf{g})n^{-\beta}
for some constant
C(\mathbf{g})>0
that depends only on
\mathbf{g}
. It implies that the functions
\mathbf{g}
belong to the Besov space
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and thus
(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}\big(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times C_{b}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}}\big)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{B}_{\infty,\infty,b}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}}
.

This lemma is proved in Section
5.2
. Since any function
\mathbf{g}
in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
can be extended on
\mathbb{R}
by functions in
C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
(again by Whitney extension theorem) and since
(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}(\xi,\mathbf{g})_{|[0,A]}=\Gamma^{-1}(\xi,\mathbf{g}_{|[0,A]})
, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 4.1
.

If the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
for some
\beta\in]0,1]
, then
\Gamma^{-1}\big(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times C^{\beta}([0,A])^{K^{2}}\big)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}([0,A])^{K^{2}}
.

Thus, we conclude that when the interaction functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
and the
L_{2}
Riesz representor
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}
are in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
for some
\beta\in]0,1]
, the functions
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
are in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty}([0,A])
which is equal to
C^{\beta}([0,A])
for
\beta\notin\mathbb{N}
.

A natural question is how to extend this result to
\beta>1
. When
\beta>1
our previous proof strategy involves higher order Palm distributions (and not only the second order), about which we are not able to say as much in terms of regularity as for the first order Palm distribution. For this reason, we change our proof strategy and we come back to the fixed point equation (
2.13
) verified by
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
. Starting from this, we show in Section
D.4
, assuming some regularity on the second order Palm distribution only (namely Conjecture
1
), that when the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
and
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}
are in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
for some
\beta>1
, then the functions
\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}
are also in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
(see corollary
D.1
).

5
Proofs of Theorem
3.2
and Lemma
4.2

5.1
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We adapt the approach of
[
14
]
, especially the proof of their theorem 2.1 . Recall (
3.12
) that defines
\mathcal{A}_{T}
, by Lemma
C.6

\Pi\big(\mathcal{A}_{T}|N\big)=1+o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)
for some
M>0
). As explained in
[
14
]
, to prove (
3.18
) it is enough to prove that the Laplace transform of
\mathcal{L}^{\Pi}\big(\sqrt{T}(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T}-\mathcal{B}_{J,T})|N\big)
conditionally on
\mathcal{A}_{T}
converges to the Laplace transform of a
\mathcal{N}(0,\|\tilde{\psi}_{L}^{0}\|_{L}^{2})
. Let
u
in a neighborhood of
0
, we set

\displaystyle I_{T}:=\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{u\sqrt{T}\big(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T}-\mathcal{B}_{J,T}\big)}\Big|N,\mathcal{A}_{T}\Big].

We have

\displaystyle I_{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{T}}e^{u\sqrt{T}(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T}-\mathcal{B}_{J,T})+L_{T}(f)-L_{T}(f^{0})}d\Pi_{f}(f)}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}_{T}}e^{L_{T}(f)-L_{T}(f^{0})}d\Pi_{f}(f)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}\frac{\int_{\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)}e^{u\sqrt{T}(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T}-\mathcal{B}_{j,T})+L_{T}(f)-L_{T}(f^{0})}d\Pi_{f|j}(f)}{\int_{\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)}e^{L_{T}(f)-L_{T}(f^{0})}d\Pi_{f|j}(f)}\Pi_{J}(j|N).

Let
j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}
and
f=(\nu,\mathbf{h})=(\nu,\varphi(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}))\in\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)
, we recall that
\tilde{f}=(\nu,\tilde{\mathbf{h}})
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}=(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}-\tilde{\mathbf{h}}^{0}).\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}+\omega_{\varphi}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}),

and Lemma
C.7
gives
\|\omega_{\varphi}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}})\|_{1}\lesssim\log(T)^{2}\varepsilon_{T}^{2}
. We rewrite the LAN expansion in terms of
\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{0}
using this linearization of
\varphi
:

\displaystyle L_{T}(f)-L_{T}(f^{0})=\sqrt{T}W_{T}((\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{0}).\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0})-\frac{T}{2}\|\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{0}\|_{L,\varphi}^{2}+R_{T,\varphi}(f),

(5.1)

with
R_{T,\varphi}(f)=R_{T}(f)+\sqrt{T}W_{T}\big(0,\omega_{\varphi}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}})\big)-\frac{T}{2}\|\omega_{\varphi}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}})\|_{L}^{2}-T\langle\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{0},(0,\omega_{\varphi}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}))\rangle_{L}
. Similarly,

\displaystyle\psi(f)-\psi(f^{0})=\langle\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}^{0},\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}\rangle_{L,\varphi}+r_{T,\varphi}(f,f^{0}),

(5.2)

with
r_{T,\varphi}(f,f^{0})=r_{T}(f,f^{0})+\langle(0,\omega_{\varphi}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}})),\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\rangle_{L}
. Now, as in the proof of Theorem 2.1 of
[
14
]
, using expansions (
5.1
) and (
5.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle u\sqrt{T}\big(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T}-\mathcal{B}_{j,T}\big)+L_{T}(f)-L_{T}(f^{0})

\displaystyle=-u\sqrt{T}\mathcal{B}_{j,T}+\frac{u^{2}}{2}\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}\|_{L,\varphi}^{2}+\sqrt{T}W_{T}((\tilde{f}_{u}-\tilde{f}^{0}).\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0})-\frac{T}{2}\|\tilde{f}_{u}-\tilde{f}^{0}\|_{L,\varphi}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+R_{T,\varphi}(f)+r_{T,\varphi}(f,f^{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)

\displaystyle=-u\sqrt{T}\mathcal{B}_{j,T}+\frac{u^{2}}{2}\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\|_{L}^{2}+L_{T}(f_{u})-L_{T}(f^{0})+(R_{T,\varphi}(f)-R_{T,\varphi}(f_{u}))+r_{T,\varphi}(f,f^{0})+o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1).

Then, using again the LAN expansion as in (
5.1
), we further have,

\displaystyle L_{T}(f_{u})+R_{T,\varphi}(f)-R_{T,\varphi}(f_{u})

\displaystyle=L_{T}(f_{u,j})+(L_{T}(f_{u})-L_{T}(f^{0}))-(L_{T}(f_{u,j})-L_{T}(f^{0}))+R_{T,\varphi}(f)-R_{T,\varphi}(f_{u})

\displaystyle=L_{T}(f_{u,j})+uW_{T}\big((\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}).\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}\big)-\frac{u^{2}}{2}\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}\|_{L,\varphi}^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+u\sqrt{T}\langle\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi},\tilde{f}-u\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}/\sqrt{T}-\tilde{f}^{0}\rangle_{L,\varphi}+R_{T,\varphi}(f)-R_{T,\varphi}(f_{u,j}).

In addition, by orthogonality,

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\langle\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi},\tilde{f}-u\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}/\sqrt{T}-\tilde{f}^{0}\rangle_{L,\varphi}+W_{T}\big((\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}).\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}\big)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{T}\langle\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi},\tilde{f}^{0}-\tilde{f}^{0,j}\rangle_{L,\varphi}+W_{T}\big((\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}).\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}\big)=\sqrt{T}\mathcal{B}_{j,T}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle u\sqrt{T}\big(\psi(f)-\hat{\psi}_{T}-\mathcal{B}_{j,T}\big)+L_{T}(f)-L_{T}(f^{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{u^{2}}{2}\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\|_{L}^{2}+L_{T}(f_{u,j})-L_{T}(f^{0})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\big(R_{T,\varphi}(f)-R_{T,\varphi}(f_{u,j})\big)+u\sqrt{T}r_{T}(f,f^{0})+\frac{u^{2}}{2}\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}\|_{L,\varphi}^{2}+o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1).

By Lemma
E.2
, we have
\underset{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}{\max}\underset{f\in\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)}{\sup}|R_{T,\varphi}(f)-R_{T,\varphi}(f_{u,j})|=o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)
, and with similar computations to (
C.8
), one can show that

\displaystyle\underset{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}{\max}\|\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L,\varphi}-\tilde{\psi}^{0,j}_{L,\varphi}\|_{L,\varphi}^{2}\lesssim\underset{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}{\max}\|\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0}-P^{j}_{2}(\mathbf{g}^{0}_{L}./\boldsymbol{\bar{\varphi}}^{0})\|_{2}^{2},

the last term being a
o(1)
by assumption. Moreover, since
\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}
is the least favourable direction and by Lemma
C.7
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle(0,\omega_{\varphi}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}})),\tilde{\psi}^{0}_{L}\rangle_{L}|=|\langle\omega_{\varphi}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}),\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}\rangle_{2}|\leq\|\mathbf{g}^{0}_{2}\|_{\infty}\|\omega_{\varphi}(\tilde{\mathbf{h}})\|_{1}\lesssim\log(T)^{2}\varepsilon_{T}^{2},

and since
\log(T)^{2}\sqrt{T}\varepsilon_{T}^{2}\rightarrow 0
, we find that
\sup\big\{\sqrt{T}r_{T,\varphi}(f,f^{0}),f\in\mathcal{A}_{T}\big\}=o(1)
. Combining these results together, we obtain

\displaystyle I_{T}=e^{o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)+\frac{u^{2}}{2}\|\tilde{\psi}_{L}^{0}\|_{L}^{2}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}_{T}}\frac{\int_{\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)}e^{L_{T}(f_{u,j})}d\Pi_{f|j}(f)}{\int_{\mathcal{A}_{T}(j)}e^{L_{T}(f)}d\Pi_{f|j}(f)}\Pi_{J}(j|N),

and with condition (
3.17
) we finally have
I_{T}=e^{o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)+\frac{u^{2}}{2}\|\tilde{\psi}_{L}^{0}\|_{L}^{2}}
, which concludes the proof.

5.2
Proof of Lemma
4.2

Let
(\xi,\mathbf{g})=(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})
with
(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}}
, we first show that
\|\mathbf{g}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}\|_{\infty}\lesssim C(\mathbf{g})n^{-\beta}
for some
C(\mathbf{g})>0
(that depends only on
\mathbf{g}
). Note that since
C^{\beta}_{b}(\mathbb{R})\subset L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we have
\mathbf{g}\in L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})^{K^{2}}
by Lemma
4.1
. Recall that
\mathcal{K}_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{g}}:=(\mathcal{K}_{n}\tilde{g}_{l,k},(l,k)\in[K]^{2})
and we set
\mathcal{K}_{n}(\tilde{\xi},\tilde{\mathbf{g}}):=(\tilde{\xi},\mathcal{K}_{n}\tilde{\mathbf{g}})
. By Lemma
4.1
,
(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}
is a bounded operator on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{\infty}^{K^{2}}(\mathbb{R})
and thus we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{g}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})-(\xi,\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g})\|_{\infty}=\|(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-(\Gamma^{e})^{-1}\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})-\mathcal{K}_{n}(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})+\mathcal{K}_{n}(\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{g}^{\prime})-\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{g}^{\prime}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{K}_{n}\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-\beta}+\|\mathcal{K}_{n}\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g})\|_{\infty},

where the last inequality comes from the
\beta
-Hölder assumption on
\mathbf{g}^{\prime}
. Now, using the expression of
\Gamma^{e}
given in Lemma
4.1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}_{n}\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\Gamma^{e}(\xi,\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g})\|_{\infty}\leq\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}\bigg|\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\lambda_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]\bigg|

(5.3)

\displaystyle+\underset{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}{\max}\mu_{l}^{0}\big\|\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{l,k}^{e}g_{l,k})-p_{l,k}^{e}\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{l,k}\big\|_{\infty}+\underset{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}{\max}\mu_{l}^{0}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\big\|\mathcal{K}_{n}\zeta^{e}_{l,j,k}(g_{j,k})-\zeta_{l,j,k}^{e}(\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{j,k})\big\|_{\infty}.

For the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.3
), for all
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
, let
\bar{p}_{l,k}(x):=p^{e}_{l,k}(x)\mathds{1}_{x\in[0,A]}
. Using formula (
A.1
) in the appendix, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\lambda_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\int_{0}^{A}(g_{l,k}-\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{l,k})(A-s)\frac{dN_{s}^{l}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mu^{0}_{l}\int_{0}^{A}g_{l,k}(s)p_{l,k}(s)ds-\mu^{0}_{l}\int_{0}^{A}(\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{l,k})(u)p_{l,k}(s)ds

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mu^{0}_{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{l,k}(s)\bar{p}_{l,k}(s)ds-\mu^{0}_{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{l,k})(s)\bar{p}_{l,k}(s)ds

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mu^{0}_{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{l,k}(s)\bar{p}_{l,k}(s)ds-\mu^{0}_{l}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{l,k}(s)\mathcal{K}_{n}\bar{p}_{l,k}(s)ds,

where for the last equality we have used that
\mathcal{K}
is symmetric about
0
. As the functions
\bar{p}_{l,k}
are in
C^{\beta}([0,A])
, as shown in Lemma
D.3
, in Section
D.3
and equal to
0
elsewhere and since
\mathcal{K}
is supported on
[-1,1]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}\bigg|\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\lambda_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]\bigg|\lesssim\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\|g_{l,k}\|_{\infty}\|\bar{p}_{l,k}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\bar{p}_{l,k}\|_{1}\lesssim\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\infty}\big(n^{-\beta}+n^{-1}\big).

For second term on the right-hand side of (
5.3
), since
\mathcal{K}
is supported on
[-1,1]
and since the functions
(p_{l,k}^{e},(l,k)\in[K]^{2})
are in
C^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})
, we have for any
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{l,k}^{e}g_{l,k})(x)-p_{l,k}^{e}(x)\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{l,k}(x)|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{-1}^{1}\mathcal{K}(u)g_{l,k}(x-u/n)\big(p_{l,k}^{e}(x-u/n)-p_{l,k}^{e}(x)\big)du\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{-\beta}\|\mathcal{K}\|_{1}\|g_{l,k}\|_{\infty}

It proves that
\underset{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}{\max}\mu_{l}^{0}\big\|\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{l,k}^{e}g_{l,k})-p_{l,k}^{e}\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{l,k}\big\|_{\infty}\lesssim n^{-\beta}\|\mathcal{K}\|_{1}\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\infty}
.
It remains to study the third term on the right-hand side of (
5.3
). For this, recall the definition of
\lambda_{t}^{k,e}
in (
4.1
) and we set
\Delta_{u/n}(k,x,l)=\lambda_{A}^{k,e}(f^{0}_{k},(x,l))-\lambda_{A-u/n}^{k,e}(f^{0}_{k},(x,l))
( as in (
D.2
) in the proof of Lemma
D.3
). Let
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
l\in[K]
. To shorten computations, we set for
u\in[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle v_{u/n}^{(x,l)}(j,k):=\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(x,l)}\bigg[\int\big(\mathds{1}_{s\in[-\frac{u}{n},A-\frac{u}{n}]}-\mathds{1}_{s\in[0,A]}\big)\mathds{1}_{(s,j)\neq(x,l)}g_{j,k}(A-s-\frac{u}{n})\frac{dN_{s}^{j}}{\lambda_{A}^{k,e}(f^{0}_{k},(x,l))}\bigg],

\displaystyle w_{u/n}^{(x,l)}(j,k):=\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(x,l)}\bigg[\int_{-\frac{u}{n}}^{A-\frac{u}{n}}\mathds{1}_{(s,j)\neq(x,l)}g_{j,k}(A-s-\frac{u}{n})\bar{\Delta}_{u/n}(k,x,l)dN_{s}^{j}\bigg],

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}_{u/n}(k,x,l):=\frac{\Delta_{u/n}(k,x,l)}{\big(\lambda_{A-\frac{u}{n}}^{k}(f_{0}^{k})+h^{0,e}_{l,k}(A-x-\frac{u}{n})\mathds{1}_{x+u/n\notin[0,A[}\big)\big(\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})+h^{0,e}_{l,k}(A-x)\mathds{1}_{x\notin[0,A[}\big)}.

With Fubini theorem and formula (
A.3
), we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{n}\zeta_{l,j,k}^{e}(g_{j,k})(x)-\zeta_{l,j,k}^{e}(\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{j,k})(x)

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{K}(u)\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(x+\frac{u}{n},l)}\bigg[\int_{0}^{A}\mathds{1}_{(s,j)\neq(x+\frac{u}{n},l)}g_{j,k}(A-s)\frac{dN_{s}^{j}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f_{0}^{k})+h^{0,e}_{l,k}(A-x-\frac{u}{n})\mathds{1}_{x+u/n\notin[0,A[}}\bigg]du

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(x,l)}\bigg[\int_{0}^{A}\mathds{1}_{(s,j)\neq(x,l)}\int\mathcal{K}(u)g_{j,k}(A-s-\frac{u}{n})du\frac{dN_{s}^{j}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})+h^{0,e}_{l,k}(A-x)\mathds{1}_{x\notin[0,A[}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{K}(u)\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(x,l)}\bigg[\int_{-\frac{u}{n}}^{A-\frac{u}{n}}\mathds{1}_{(s,j)\neq(x,l)}g_{j,k}(A-s-\frac{u}{n})\frac{dN_{s}^{j}}{\lambda_{A-\frac{u}{n}}^{k}(f_{0}^{k})+h^{0,e}_{l,k}(A-x-\frac{u}{n})\mathds{1}_{x+u/n\notin[0,A[}}\bigg]du

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\int\mathcal{K}(u)\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(x,l)}\bigg[\int_{0}^{A}\mathds{1}_{(s,j)\neq(x,l)}g_{j,k}(A-s-\frac{u}{n})\frac{dN_{s}^{j}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})+h^{0,e}_{l,k}(A-x)\mathds{1}_{x\notin[0,A[}}\bigg]du

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{K}(u)\big(v_{u/n}^{(x,l)}(j,k)+w_{u/n}^{(x,l)}(j,k)\big)du.

Let
u\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle|v_{u/n}^{(x,l)}(j,k)|\lesssim\|g_{j,k}\|_{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(x,l)}\big[N^{j}([-u/n,0]\backslash\{x\})+N^{j}([A-u/n,A]\backslash\{x\})\big]\lesssim n^{-1}\|g_{j,k}\|_{\infty},

where for the last inequality inequality we have used Lemma
D.1
. We turn to
w
. Since the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0,e}
are
\beta
Holder, proceeding as in the proof of Lemma
D.3
, for
u\in[0,1]
we find that under
\mathbb{P}^{(x,l)}
:

\displaystyle\big|\bar{\Delta}_{u/n}(k,x,l)\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\big|\Delta_{u/n}(k,x,l)\big|\lesssim n^{-\beta}+n^{-\beta}\underbrace{\Big(\sum_{i\neq l}N^{i}([0,A-u/n])+N^{l}([0,A-u/n]\backslash\{x\})\Big)}_{C^{1}_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt+\underbrace{\sum_{i\neq l}N^{i}\big([-u/n,0]\cup[A-u/n,A]\big)+N^{l}\big(([-u/n,0]\cup[A-u/n,A])\backslash\{x\}\big)}_{C^{2}_{n}}.

Thus, using this time the bound (
A.4
) on the first Palm second moment measure, we obtain

\displaystyle|w_{u/n}^{(x,l)}(j,k)|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbf{g}_{j,k}\|_{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(x,l)}\bigg[N^{j}\big([-u/n,A-u/n]\backslash\{x\}\big)\Big(n^{-\beta}+n^{-\beta}C^{1}_{n}+C^{2}_{n}\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathbf{g}_{j,k}\|_{\infty}(n^{-\beta}+n^{-1}).

By doing the same for
u\in[-1,0]
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\underset{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}{\max}\mu_{l}^{0}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\big\|\mathcal{K}_{n}\zeta^{e}_{l,j,k}(g_{j,k})-\zeta_{l,j,k}^{e}(\mathcal{K}_{n}g_{j,k})\big\|_{\infty}\lesssim n^{-\beta}\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\infty}\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\infty}.

and it allows to conclude that
\|\mathbf{g}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}\|_{\infty}\leq C(\mathbf{g})n^{-\beta}
for some constant
C(\mathbf{g})>0
that depends only on
\mathbf{g}
.

Now, we prove that it implies that the functions
\mathbf{g}
belong to
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty,b}(\mathbb{R})
(recall that we already now that the functions
\mathbf{g}
are in
L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
) . To do so, because
\|\mathbf{g}-\mathcal{K}_{n}\mathbf{g}\|_{\infty}\lesssim C(\mathbf{g})n^{-\beta}
, we can apply the proposition 2.3 of
[
38
]
(taking
p=+\infty
) to the functions
\mathbf{g}
and it gives that these functions are in
\mathcal{B}^{\beta}_{\infty,\infty,b}(\mathbb{R})
(condition (b) of this proposition is verified because we work with a kernel of the form
\mathcal{K}(x-y)
with
\mathcal{K}
continuous). It concludes the proof of the lemma.

A
Palm distributions

In this appendix, we briefly introduce Palm calculus and we state the results used in Section
2
and Section
4
, giving each time references for the proofs and for further details. Palm theory is formally presented in the general case by, among others,
[
20
]
(chapter 13),
[
37
]
(chapter 7) and
[
12
]
(chapters 7 and 8), we mainly use the terminology of
[
20
]
. For simplicity and because it is sufficient for our purpose, we introduce Palm distributions for a
K
-marked and boundedly finite point process
N
on
\mathbb{R}
. In this section, to avoid confusion, we will write
N(dk\times dx)
instead of
dN^{k}_{x}
which is used elsewhere in the paper. Let’s denote by
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}\times[K]}^{\#}
the set of counting measures on
\mathbb{R}
with marks in
[K]
(equipped with its Borel
\sigma
-field induced by the "weak hash" topology, see section 9.1 of
[
20
]
and appendix A2.6 of
[
19
]
). Assume that the point process
N
has a
\sigma
-finite first moment measure denoted
M_{N}
. The Campbell measure
C_{N}
is a measure on
\mathbb{R}\times[K]\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}\times[K]}^{\#}
(equipped with its product
\sigma
-field ) defined
by

\displaystyle C_{N}(B\times k\times U)=\mathbb{E}\big[N^{k}(B)\mathds{1}_{N\in U}\big].

For any measurable function
\Phi:\mathbb{R}\times[K]\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}\times[K]}^{\#}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
we have

\displaystyle C_{N}\Phi:=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int\Phi(x,k,N)N(dk\times dx)\bigg]=\int\Phi(x,k,\bar{N})C_{N}(dk\times dx\times d\bar{N}).

The Palm kernel
(\mathbb{P}^{(x,k)},(x,k)\in\mathbb{R}\times[K])
is then defined as the kernel from
\mathbb{R}\times[K]
to
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}\times[K]}^{\#}
obtained by disintegrating the Campbell measure with respect to the first moment measure
M_{N}
and we have

\displaystyle C_{N}\Phi=\int\mathbb{E}^{(x,k)}[\Phi(x,k,N)]M_{N}(dk\times dx),

(A.1)

\mathbb{E}^{(x,k)}
being an expectation under the Palm distribution
\mathbb{P}^{(x,k)}
; see proposition 13.1.IV and the preceding discussion in
[
20
]
. When
N
is simple, which we consider it the case from now on,
\mathbb{P}^{(x,k)}
can be interpreted as the conditional distribution of
N
given that there is a point at
x
with mark
k
(the latter event being of probability
0
in general, this only holds approximately, see
[
37
]
section 6.6 for further details). In particular,
\mathbb{P}^{(x,k)}\big(N^{k}(\{x\})=1\big)=1
. By disintegrating the second order Campbell measure
C^{2}_{N}
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[K]^{2}\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}\times[K]}^{\#}
by

\displaystyle C^{2}_{N}(B_{1}\times B_{2}\times k_{1}\times k_{2}\times U)=\mathbb{E}[N^{k_{1}}(B_{1})N^{k_{2}}(B_{2})\mathds{1}_{N\in U}],

with respect to the second moment measure (when it is
\sigma
-finite), we obtain the second order Palm kernel
(\mathbb{P}^{(x_{1},x_{2},k_{1},k_{2})},(x_{1},x_{2},k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[K]^{2})
. Because of this, the kernel
(\mathbb{P}^{(x,k)},(x,k)\in\mathbb{R}\times[K])
is sometimes called the first order Palm kernel. Then, for any measurable function
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[K]^{2}\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{R}\times[K]}^{\#}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
we have

\displaystyle C_{N}^{2}\Phi=\int\mathbb{E}^{(x_{1},x_{2},k_{1},k_{2})}[\Phi(x_{1},x_{2},k_{1},k_{2},N)]M_{N}(dk_{1}\times dk_{2}\times dx_{1}\times dx_{2}).

The second order Palm distributions are symmetric in the sense that
\mathbb{P}^{(x_{1},x_{2},k_{1},k_{2})}=\mathbb{P}^{(x_{2},x_{1},k_{2},k_{1})}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{P}^{(x_{1},k_{1},x_{2},k_{2})}\big(N^{k_{1}}(\{x_{1}\})=1,N^{k_{2}}(\{x_{2}\})=1\big)=1
(see Lemma 6.2 of
[
37
]
).
By the
iteration principles
(see corollary 6.24 of
[
37
]
or alternatively
[
36
]
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(x_{1},k_{1})}\bigg[\int\Phi(x_{2},k_{2},N)N(dk_{2}\times dx_{2})\bigg]=\int\mathbb{E}^{(x_{1},x_{2},k_{1},k_{2})}\big[\Phi(x_{2},k_{2},N)\big]M_{N}^{(x_{1},k_{1})}(dk_{2}\times dx_{2}),

(A.2)

where
M_{N}^{(x_{1},k_{1})}
is the Palm first moment measure:
M_{N}^{(x_{1},k_{1})}(B\times l)=\mathbb{E}^{(x_{1},k_{1})}[N^{l}(B)]
.

Assume now that the point process
N
is stationary and denote by
S_{x}N
the point process
N
whose points are all shifted by
x
. In this case, the Palm distributions are all equal up to a shift (
[
20
]
- Section 13.4):

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{(x,k)}(N\in.)=\mathbb{P}^{(0,k)}(S_{x}N\in.).

(A.3)

If
N
has a
(n+1)
-th moment measure, the
n
-th moment measure of the Palm distribution is well defined and is linked to the reduced
(n+1)
-th moment measure
\bar{M}^{(n+1)}
of
N
, see proposition 13.2.VI of
[
20
]
. Specializing to the case of a linear multivariate stationary Hawkes process
N
with parameters
f^{0}\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
, Lemma
D.1
shows that for the Palm first moment measure we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(0,l)}[N^{k}(B)]=\mathds{1}_{0\in B,l=k}+\int_{B}m_{l,k}(u)du.

Then, let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be two bounded measurable sets and
(l,k_{1},k_{2})\in[K]^{3}
such that either
0\notin B_{1}
or
l\neq k_{1}
, then we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(0,l)}\big[N^{k_{1}}(B_{1})N^{k_{2}}(B_{2})\big]\lesssim|B_{1}|\mathds{1}_{l=k_{2},0\in B_{2}}+|B_{1}\cap B_{2}|\mathds{1}_{k_{1}=k_{2}}+|B_{1}||B_{2}|.

(A.4)

Similarly, let
B
be a bounded measurable set and
(x,l)\neq(s,j)
, if either
x\notin B
or
l\neq k
and if either
s\notin B
or
j\neq k
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}^{(x,s,l,j)}[N^{k}(B)]\lesssim|B|.

(A.5)

Finally, even if we do not use it in this paper, we point out that for a stationary Hawkes process, by Theorem 8.4.18 of
[
12
]
, the Palm distribution
\mathbb{P}^{(0,k)}
is equal to the convolution between the stationary distribution of the process and a "reweighted and shifted" version of the distribution of a cluster rooted in
(0,k)
. This result is stated in more general terms in Lemma 6.16 in
[
37
]
and has a generalisation to higher order Palm distributions, see Theorem 6.30 in
[
37
]
or Section 5 in
[
36
]
.

B
Proofs of section
2

In this section, we prove the results of Section
2
on semiparametric efficiency. We first prove Lemma
2.1
on the equivalence between the norms
\|.\|_{L}
and
\|.\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}
. Then, we prove the results on the LAN expansion (Lemma
2.2
and Lemma
2.3
).

B.1
Proof of Lemma
2.1

It is clear that
\langle\cdot{,}\cdot\rangle_{L}
is a bilinear map, symmetric, positive semi-definite on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
and
\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}=\langle(\xi,\mathbf{g}),(\xi,\mathbf{g})\rangle_{L}
. We first show that there exist two positive constants
C_{2}>C_{1}
such that for all
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
,

\displaystyle\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}\leq C_{2}\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})},

(B.1)

\displaystyle C_{1}\|\mathbf{g}\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}\leq\|\mathbf{g}\|_{L}.

(B.2)

We recall the definition of these norms:

\displaystyle\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\xi_{k}^{2}+\sum_{l=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}\frac{g^{2}_{l,k}(x)}{\nu_{k}^{0}+h_{l,k}^{0}(x)}dx\hskip 7.11317pt\text{and}\hskip 7.11317pt\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big].

We begin by (
B.1
), fix
k\in[K]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big]\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{1}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]\xi_{k}^{2}+2\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg].

For the second therm on the right-hand side, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\frac{\big(\sum_{l=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}g_{l,k}(A-t)dN_{t}^{l}\big)^{2}}{\nu^{0}_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}h_{j,k}^{0}(A-u)dN_{u}^{l}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{K-1}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{0}^{A}\frac{|g_{l,k}(A-t)|}{\sqrt{\nu^{0}_{k}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-t)}}\times\frac{\sqrt{\nu^{0}_{k}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-t)}}{\sqrt{\nu^{0}_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}h_{j,k}^{0}(A-u)dN_{u}^{l}}}dN_{t}^{l}\bigg)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{K-1}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{0}^{A}\frac{|g_{l,k}(A-t)|}{\sqrt{\nu^{0}_{k}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-t)}}dN_{t}^{l}\bigg)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{0}^{A}\frac{|g_{l,k}(A-t)|}{\sqrt{\nu^{0}_{k}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-t)}}\big(dN_{t}^{l}-\lambda_{t}^{l}(f^{0}_{l})dt\big)\bigg)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\bigg(\int_{0}^{A}\frac{|g_{l,k}(A-t)|}{\sqrt{\nu^{0}_{k}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-t)}}\lambda_{t}^{l}(f^{0}_{l})dt\bigg)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{A}\frac{g_{l,k}(A-t)^{2}}{\nu^{0}_{k}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-t)}\lambda_{t}^{l}(f^{0}_{l})dt\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[A\int_{0}^{A}\frac{g_{l,k}(A-t)^{2}}{\nu^{0}_{k}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-t)}\lambda_{t}^{l}(f^{0}_{l})^{2}dt\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{K}\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}\bigg(\mathbb{E}_{0}[\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})]+A\mathbb{E}_{0}\big[\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})^{2}\big]\bigg)\sum_{l=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}\frac{g_{l,k}(A-t)^{2}}{\nu_{k}^{0}+h^{0}_{l,k}(A-t)}dt

\displaystyle=2^{K}\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}\bigg(\mathbb{E}_{0}[\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})]+A\mathbb{E}_{0}\big[\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})^{2}\big]\bigg)\|\mathbf{g}_{k}\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}^{2},

where for the penultimate inequality we have used Jensen inequality for the second term. So we have shown (
B.1
) with

\displaystyle C_{2}^{2}=2\max\Big(\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[1/\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})\big]\hskip 2.84544pt;\hskip 2.84544pt2^{K}\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}\big(\mathbb{E}_{0}[\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})]+A\mathbb{E}_{0}\big[\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})^{2}\big]\big)\Big).

For the inequality (
B.2
), we adapt the proof of Lemma 4 from the supplementary material of
[
21
]
to our purpose. We define for all
l\in[K]
the event

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{l}=\{N([-A,0])=0\text{ and }N(]0,A])=N^{l}(]0,\mathcal{A}])=1\}.

The events
(\mathcal{A}_{l})_{l\in[K]}
are disjoints. On an event
\mathcal{A}_{l}
, we denote by
U_{l}
the unique point of the process of type
l
between
[0,A]
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\frac{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}g_{j,k}(A-t)dN_{t}^{j}\Big)^{2}}{\nu_{k}^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}h_{j,k}^{0}(A-t)dN_{t}^{j}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\mathds{1}\Big\{\bigcup_{l=1}^{K}\mathcal{A}_{l}\Big\}\times\frac{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}g_{j,k}(A-t)dN_{t}^{j}\Big)^{2}}{\nu_{k}^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}h_{j,k}^{0}(A-t)dN_{t}^{j}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\mathds{1}(\mathcal{A}_{l})\frac{\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}g_{j,k}(A-t)dN_{t}^{j}\Big)^{2}}{\nu_{k}^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}h_{j,k}^{0}(A-t)dN_{t}^{j}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\mathds{1}(\mathcal{A}_{l})\frac{g_{l,k}(A-U_{l})^{2}}{\nu_{k}^{0}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-U_{l})}\Bigg].

Furthermore, as in proposition 7.3.III of
[
19
]
, let
\mathds{Q}
be the
K
-marked process consisting in
K
independent component each being a Poisson process on
[0;+\infty[
with constant intensity equal to
1
, the likelihood ratio of the Hawkes process relative to
\mathds{Q}
on
[-A,A]
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{A}=\exp\bigg(2AK+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{-A}^{A}\log(\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k}))dN_{t}^{k}-\int_{-A}^{A}\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})dt\bigg).

On the event
\mathcal{A}_{l}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{A}

\displaystyle=\exp(2KA)\times\lambda_{U_{l}}^{l}(f^{0})\times\exp\bigg(-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\Big(2A\nu^{0}_{k}+\int_{U_{l}}^{A}h_{l,k}^{0}(t-U_{l})dt\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\nu_{l}^{0}\exp\Big(-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\big(2A\nu_{0}^{k}+\|h^{0}_{l,k}\|_{1}\big)\Big):=r_{l},

and
r_{l}>0
because the constraint
\rho(\mathbf{h}^{0})<1
implies that the functions
(h^{0}_{l,k})_{l,k}
are integrable. Moreover, under the distribution
\mathds{Q}
and conditionally on
\mathcal{A}_{l}
,
U_{l}
is distributed uniformly over
[0,A]
so we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(0,\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\mathds{Q}}\Bigg[\mathcal{L}_{A}\mathds{1}(\mathcal{A}_{l})\frac{g_{l,k}(A-U_{l})^{2}}{\nu_{k}^{0}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-U_{l})}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{l=1}^{K}r_{l}\mathbb{E}_{\mathds{Q}}\Bigg[\mathds{1}(\mathcal{A}_{l})\frac{g_{l,k}(A-U_{l})^{2}}{\nu_{k}^{0}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-U_{l})}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{l=1}^{K}r_{l}\mathds{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{l})\int_{0}^{A}\frac{g_{l,k}(A-u)^{2}}{\nu_{k}^{0}+h_{l,k}^{0}(A-u)}du\geq\underset{l\in[K]}{\min}r_{l}\mathds{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{l})\times\|\mathbf{g}_{k}\|^{2}_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}.

Finally, choosing
C_{1}^{2}=\min\{r_{l}\mathds{Q}(\mathcal{A}_{l}),l\in[K]\}>0
, we have proved (
B.2
). This shows that
\|.\|_{L}
is a norm on
L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
equivalent to
\|.\|_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
. For
\|.\|_{L}
to be a norm on the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
, it remains to prove the positive definiteness. Suppose that
\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}=0
,

\displaystyle\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}=0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Bigg]=0

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\mathds{1}\Big\{N([0,A])=0\Big\}\times\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Bigg]=0

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\mathds{1}\Big\{N([0,A])=0\Big\}\times\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\xi_{k}^{2}}{\nu^{0}_{k}}\Bigg]=0

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{P}_{0}\big(N([0,A])=0\big)\times\sum_{k=1}^{K}\xi_{k}^{2}=0\implies\xi=0,

because
\mathbb{P}_{0}\big(N([0,A]=0\big)>0
. Whence,
\|\mathbf{g}\|_{L}=\|(0,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}=0
and since
\|.\|_{L}
is a norm on
L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
,
\mathbf{g}=0
necessarily. The positive definiteness over
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
is thus proved.

So far, we have shown that
\|.\|_{L}
is a norm on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
(and
\langle\cdot{,}\cdot\rangle_{L}
an inner product) and that this norm is equivalent to
\|.\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})}
on
L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
. To conclude, we extend the equivalence of norms on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
. By a direct corollary of Banach-Schauder theorem, if
(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}},\|.\|_{L})
is complete, then the domination (
B.1
) implies the equivalence of norms over
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
. But, by definition of the LAN norm,
(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}},\|.\|_{L})
is complete if and only if
(\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}),\|.\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0})})
is complete. In addition, the positive definiteness of the LAN norm shows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\big(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}\big)=\mathcal{S}\big(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times\{0\}\big)\oplus\mathcal{S}\big(\{0\}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}\big),

and the equivalence of norms on
L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
implies that
\mathcal{S}\big(\{0\}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}\big)
is a close subspace of
L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
. Consequently, as a sum of a close subspace and a subspace of finite dimension,
\mathcal{S}\big(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}\big)
is a close subspace of
L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
and so it is a complete space, which in turns prove the equivalence of the norms on
\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2}^{K^{2}}0
.

In conclusion, it comes directly with the previous results that
\mathcal{S}
is a bijective, bounded, linear operator between the Hilbert spaces
(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}},\|.\|_{(2,\mathbf{h}^{0})})
and
(\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}),\|.\|_{L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{0})})
.

B.2
Proofs of the LAN expansions

In this section, we prove the results on the LAN expansion, we begin with the expansion in model
\mathcal{P}
(Lemma
2.2
) and then we extend it to the model
\mathcal{P}_{R}
(Lemma
2.3
).

We will use use in the proof the expansion (
2.1
) that we first detail here. Let
(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})\in]0,+\infty[^{K}\times\mathcal{H}
and
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
such that for
T
large enough,
\mathbf{h}^{0}+\mathbf{g}/\sqrt{T}\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

\displaystyle L_{T}\Big(\nu^{0}+\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{T}},\mathbf{h}^{0}+\frac{\mathbf{g}}{\sqrt{T}}\Big)-L_{T}\Big(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0}\big)

(B.3)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\log\bigg(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg)dN_{t}^{k}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\hskip-2.84544pt\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\big(dN_{t}^{k}\hskip-1.42271pt-\hskip-1.42271pt\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})dt\big)-\frac{1}{2T}\hskip-2.84544pt\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{T}\hskip-2.84544pt\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}R\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big)\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}

\displaystyle=W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\frac{1}{2}\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}+R_{T}\big(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{T}},\frac{\mathbf{g}}{\sqrt{T}}\big),

with

\displaystyle R_{T}\big(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{T}},\frac{\mathbf{g}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)=\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}R\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big)\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})},

(B.4)

\displaystyle\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})=\frac{1}{2}\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}-\frac{1}{2T}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})},

and
R
defined for
x>-1
by
\log(1+x)=x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}+x^{2}R(x)
. With (
B.3
), we begin by proving the LAN property when the functions
h_{l,k}^{0}
are bounded away from
0
. Then, we show that this proof can be quite directly extended to the general case.

When the functions
h_{l,k}^{0}
are bounded away from
0
,
L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+}=L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
and let
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
. We study the behavior of the log-likelihood along the nonlinear path
(\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}}
defined by (
2.3
). Because
a_{T}^{-1}\arctan(g_{l,k}a_{T}/\sqrt{T})
is bounded by
\pi/2a_{T}\rightarrow 0
, the functions
h^{0}_{l,k}+a_{T}^{-1}\arctan(g_{l,k}a_{T}/\sqrt{T})
are non-negative for
T
large enough and this path is equal to (
2.9
):

\displaystyle\bigg(\big(\nu_{k}^{0}+\frac{\xi_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)_{k\in[K]}\hskip 1.42271pt,\hskip 1.42271pt\Big(h_{l,k}^{0}+\frac{1}{a_{T}}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}g_{l,k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\Big)_{(l,k)\in[K]^{2}}\bigg).

Likelihood expansion.
The difference of the log-likelihoods involves the difference of the intensities and by linearity of the intensity we have:

\displaystyle\lambda_{t}^{k}\Big(\nu^{0}_{k}+\frac{\xi_{k}}{\sqrt{T}},\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\frac{1}{a_{T}}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\Big)-\lambda_{t}^{k}(\nu^{0}_{k},\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k})

(B.5)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{t}^{k}\Big(\nu^{0}_{k}+\frac{\xi_{k}}{\sqrt{T}},\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\frac{1}{a_{T}}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\Big)-\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(\nu^{0}_{k}+\frac{\xi_{k}}{\sqrt{T}},\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\frac{\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\Big)

\displaystyle+\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(\nu^{0}_{k}+\frac{\xi_{k}}{\sqrt{T}},\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\frac{\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\Big)-\lambda_{t}^{k}(\nu_{k}^{0},\mathbf{h}_{k}^{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\Big)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k}\Big).

with for
x\neq 0,\delta(x)=(\arctan(x)-x)/x
and it is continuously extended by
\delta(0)=0
. With this expression, we can expand the log-likelihood as in (
B.3
) but with some additional remainder terms coming from the nonlinearity of the path.

\displaystyle L_{T}\Big((\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}}\Big)-L_{T}(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})=W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\frac{1}{2}\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}+\bar{R}_{T}\big(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{T}},\frac{\mathbf{g}}{\sqrt{T}}\big),

(B.6)

with

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{T}\big(\frac{\xi}{\sqrt{T}},\frac{\mathbf{g}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)=\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}-\frac{1}{2T}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\Big)\big(\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}-dt\big)-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\Big)^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{2}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\Big)\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}

(B.7)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg(\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\ \xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})+\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\big)}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg)^{2}R\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0})}\bigg)\hskip 2.84544ptdN_{t}^{k}

\displaystyle:=\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,1}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,2}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,3}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,4}(\xi,\mathbf{g}).

To prove the LAN property, we first show that
\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
goes in probability to
0
, then that
W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
goes in distribution to a
\mathcal{N}\big(0,\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}\big)
and finally that the other terms
\bar{R}_{T,i}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
,
i\in[4]
, go in probability to
0
too.

Term

\mathit{\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})}
. Let
(\varepsilon_{t}^{k})_{t,k}
be a marked, stationary and predictable (with respect to the history
\mathcal{G}
) process with a first moment. Recall that the Hawkes process
N
we consider is ergodic (see proposition 12.3.IX of
[
20
]
). Fix
k\in[K]
, we know by Birkhoff ergodic theorem that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon_{t}^{k}dt\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{a.s.}\mathbb{E}_{0}[\varepsilon_{0}^{k}].

(B.8)

Lemma 2 of
[
49
]
shows that if the integrand process has a second moment then the convergence (
B.8
) holds with respect to
dN_{t}^{k}/\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})
instead of
dt
. Moreover, the convergence
\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})\rightarrow 0
is implied by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big]-\frac{1}{2T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{\mathbb{P}_{0}}0,

(B.9)

for all
k\in[K]
. Set
\varepsilon_{t}^{k}=\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}/\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})
, this process has a first moment, not necessarily a second moment, but we can still obtain the desired convergence in probability (instead of almost surely) which is sufficient for our purpose. Indeed, define for
M\geq 0
,
\varepsilon_{t,M}^{k}=\varepsilon_{t}^{k}\mathds{1}_{\varepsilon_{t}^{k}\leq M}
, this process is bounded so has a second moment and
\underset{M\rightarrow+\infty}{\lim}\varepsilon_{t,M}^{k}=\varepsilon_{t}^{k}
almost surely. Let
\eta>0
, by dominated convergence theorem, there exists
M_{0}>0
such that for
M\geq M_{0}
,
\mathbb{E}_{0}\big[|\varepsilon_{0}^{k}-\varepsilon_{0,M}^{k}|\big]\leq\eta^{2}/4
. Let
M\geq M_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon_{t}^{k}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\varepsilon_{0}^{k}]\Big|>\eta\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{0}\bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon_{t}^{k}-\varepsilon_{t,M}^{k}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big|>\frac{\eta}{2}\bigg)+\mathbb{P}_{0}\bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon_{t,M}^{k}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\varepsilon_{0,M}^{k}]\Big|>\frac{\eta}{4}\bigg).

By Markov inequality and stationarity,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon_{t}^{k}-\varepsilon_{t,M}^{k}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big|>\frac{\eta}{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\mathbb{E}_{0}\big[|\varepsilon_{0}^{k}-\varepsilon_{0,M}^{k}|\big]}{\eta}\leq\frac{\eta}{2}.

Then, because we can apply Lemma 2 of
[
49
]
to
\varepsilon^{k}_{t,M}
, there exists
T
large enough such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon_{t,M}^{k}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\varepsilon_{0,M}^{k}]\Big|>\frac{\eta}{4}\bigg)\leq\frac{\eta}{2}.

Hence, we have proved that for all
\eta>0
, there exists
T
large enough such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\varepsilon_{t}^{k}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}-\mathbb{E}_{0}[\varepsilon_{0}^{k}]\Big|>\eta\bigg)\leq\eta.

Therefore, we have obtained (
B.9
) and
\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
goes to
0
in probability.

First order term
\mathit{W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})}
. To prove the asymptotic normality of this term, we prove that the vector

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\big(dN_{t}^{k}-\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})dt\big)\bigg)_{k\in[K]}

(B.10)

converges in distribution to a centered gaussian vector with diagonal covariance matrix. To do so, we apply Theorem 2.1 of
[
48
]
. This is a slight generalisation of corollary 4.5.1 in
[
39
]
. To be consistent with
[
48
]
, for
i\in[K]
, let
H_{i}(t,k)
be the marked process defined by

H_{i}(t,k)=\begin{cases}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{i}(\xi_{i},\mathbf{g}_{i})}{\lambda_{t}^{i}(f^{0}_{i})}&\text{if}\ k=i\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

We can rewrite the
i
-th element in (
B.10
) as an integral of this marked process over
[0,T]\times[K]
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{i}(\xi_{i},\mathbf{g}_{i})}{\lambda_{t}^{i}(f^{0}_{i})}\big(dN_{t}^{i}-\lambda_{t}^{i}(f^{0}_{i})dt\big)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{[K]}H_{i}(t,k)\big(N(dt\times dk)-\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})dk\hskip 1.42271ptdt\big).

If there is convergence, the coefficient
(i,j)
of the asymptotic covariance matrix is given by the limit of

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{[K]}H_{i}(t,k)H_{j}(t,k)\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})dkdt,

as
T\rightarrow+\infty
. Obviously, for
i\neq j
, there is convergence towards
0
. By again Lemma 2 of
[
49
]
, the diagonal terms also converge and the covariance matrix is thus a diagonal matrix whose diagonal is given by

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{1}(\xi_{1},\mathbf{g}_{1})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{1}(f^{0}_{1})}\Big],\hskip 1.42271pt...\hskip 1.42271pt,\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{K}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{K})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{K}(f^{0}_{K})}\Big]\bigg).

Finally we have to verify a Lindeberg-type condition:
\forall\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\mathds{1}\Big\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}>T\epsilon\Big\}dt\Bigg]\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{}0.

By Fubini theorem, stationarity and dominated convergence theorem, this last condition is verified. Consequently, (
B.10
) tends to the announced gaussian vector and
W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
goes in distribution to a
\mathcal{N}\big(0,\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}\big)
.

Remainder terms.
We finally show that the last four terms in (
B.7
) (
\bar{R}_{T,i}(\xi,\mathbf{g}),\hskip 1.42271pti\in[4]
) go in probability to 0. These terms involve the function
\delta
. This function is continuous,
\delta(0)=0
and since
g_{l,k}<+\infty
a.s., we have that
\delta(a_{T}g_{l,k}/\sqrt{T})\rightarrow 0
a.s. . In addition,
\delta
is bounded and
\int_{0}^{A}|g_{l,k}(A-u)|dN_{u}^{l}<+\infty
a.s., for any
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
. So, by dominated convergence theorem we have:

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}\Big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\Big)=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{A}g_{l,k}(A-u)\delta\big(\frac{a_{T}g_{l,k}(A-u)}{\sqrt{T}}\big)dN_{u}^{l}\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{\textit{a.s.}}0.

(B.11)

With (
B.11
), it is clear that
\bar{R}_{T,1}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
,
\bar{R}_{T,2}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
and
\bar{R}_{T,3}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
go to
0
. For instance, consider
\bar{R}_{T,1}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
that is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\Big)\big(\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}-dt\big).

Fix
k\in[K]
, let
\epsilon>0
, by Markov inequality and stationarity we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\bigg(\Big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\Big)\big(\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}-dt\big)\Big|\geq\epsilon\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{0}\Bigg(\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\Big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\Big)\big(\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}-dt\big)\bigg)^{2}\geq T\epsilon^{2}\Bigg)\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}\big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\big)^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Bigg],

and by (
B.11
) and dominated convergence theorem, this last bound goes 0 as
T
goes to
+\infty
. Markov inequality and dominated convergence theorem also lead to the result for the following two terms (
\bar{R}_{T,2}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
and
\bar{R}_{T,3}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
).
Finally, we treat the last term
\bar{R}_{T,4}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
by reproducing some computations of the proof of Theorem 7.2 of
[
62
]
. This term is

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg(\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\ \xi_{k},\mathbf{g}_{k})+\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big(0,\mathbf{g}_{k}.\delta(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}})\big)\bigg)^{2}R\bigg(\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)}{\sqrt{T}\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg)\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}

Fix
k\in[K]
, since
\delta
is bonded by
1
, the
k
-th term of the sum is bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{4}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\hskip-5.69046pt\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{k},|\mathbf{g}_{k}|)^{2}\bigg|R\bigg(\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)}{\sqrt{T}\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg)\bigg|\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})},

which can be further bounded by

\displaystyle\underset{t_{i}\in N^{k}|_{[0,T]}}{\max}\bigg|R\bigg(\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t_{i}}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)}{\sqrt{T}\lambda_{t_{i}}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg)\bigg|\frac{4}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}^{k}_{t}(\xi_{k},|\mathbf{g}_{k}|)^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}.

Thanks to (
B.9
) and because
R(x)\xrightarrow[x\rightarrow 0]{}0
, it is enough to show that

\displaystyle\underset{t_{i}\in N^{k}|_{[0,T]}}{\max}\bigg|\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t_{i}}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)}{\sqrt{T}\lambda_{t_{i}}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg|\xrightarrow[T\rightarrow+\infty]{\mathbb{P}}0.

Let
\epsilon>0
, again by Markov inequality and stationarity,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\Bigg(\underset{t_{i}\in N^{k}|_{[0,T]}}{\max}\bigg|\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t_{i}}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)}{\sqrt{T}\lambda_{t_{i}}^{k}(f^{0})}\bigg|>\epsilon\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{0}\Bigg(\underset{t_{i}\in N^{k}|_{[0,T]}}{\max}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t_{i}}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)^{2}}{\lambda_{t_{i}}^{k}(f^{0})^{2}}\mathds{1}\Big\{\bigg|\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t_{i}}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)}{\lambda_{t_{i}}^{k}(f^{0})}\bigg|>\sqrt{T}\epsilon\Big\}>T\epsilon^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{0}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\Big(\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\mathds{1}\Big\{\ \bigg|\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg|>\sqrt{T}\epsilon\Big\}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}>T\epsilon^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\mathds{1}\big\{\ \bigg|\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}\big(\xi_{k},a_{T}^{-1}\arctan\big(\frac{a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}}{\sqrt{T}}\big)\big)}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\Big|>\sqrt{T}\epsilon\bigg\}\Bigg],

and the last term goes to
0
as
T\rightarrow+\infty
by dominated convergence theorem which terminates to prove that
\bar{R}_{T,4}(\xi,\mathbf{g})\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0
. Hence, we have shown that the model
\mathcal{P}
has the LAN property at
f^{0}
when the functions
h^{0}_{l,k}
are bounded away from
0
.

Extension to the general case.
We no longer assume that the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are bounded away from
0
. Previously, this assumption has only been used to show that the path (
2.3
) is in fact equal to the path (
2.9
) for
T
large enough, and from that we have obtained the expansion (
B.6
). This assumption was not used to study the asymptotic behavior of the terms of the expansion (
B.6
), namely
W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
,
\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
and
\bar{R}_{T,i}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
(
i\in[4])
. Lemma
B.1
shows that, without assuming the the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are bounded away from
0
, we still have for
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+}

\displaystyle L_{T}\Big(\big(\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T)\big)_{\mathcal{P}}\Big)-L_{T}(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})

\displaystyle=W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\frac{1}{2}\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}+\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,1}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,2}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,3}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1),

and therefore the LAN expansion is proved in the general case for model
\mathcal{P}_{l}
.
∎

The following lemma is a technical result used in the previous proof when it is not assumed that the functions
\mathbf{h}^{0}
are bounded away from
0
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}^{+}
,

\displaystyle L_{T}\Big(\big(\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T)\big)_{\mathcal{P}}\Big)-L_{T}(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})

\displaystyle=W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\frac{1}{2}\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}+\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})

\displaystyle\hskip 22.76228pt+\bar{R}_{T,1}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,2}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,3}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)

To shorten the notations in this proof, we write
\xi_{T}
for
\xi/\sqrt{T}
and
\mathbf{g}_{T}
for
a_{T}^{-1}\arctan(a_{T}\mathbf{g}/\sqrt{T})
and we define the vector of
K
functions
(\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}}
by

\displaystyle\big((\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}}\big)_{l}(x)=(h^{0}_{l,k}(x)+g_{T,l,k}(x))\mathds{1}_{h^{0}_{l,k}(x)<-g_{T,l,k}(x)}.

First, note that

\lambda_{t}^{k}\big(\big(\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T)\big)_{\mathcal{P}}\big)=\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\nu^{0}_{k}+\xi_{T,k},\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})-\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big(0,(\mathbf{h}^{0}_{K}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}}\big).

Then, reproducing the computations that lead to the expansion (
B.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle L_{T}\Big(\big(\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T)\big)_{\mathcal{P}}\Big)-L_{T}(\nu^{0},\mathbf{h}^{0})=W_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})-\frac{1}{2}\|(\xi,\mathbf{g})\|_{L}^{2}+\Delta Q_{T}(\xi,\mathbf{g})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\bar{R}_{T,1}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,2}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,3}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,1}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,2}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,3}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})+\bar{R}_{T,4}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g}),

with

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{T,1}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(0,(\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\big(dN_{t}^{k}-\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})\big)

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{T,2}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(0,(\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{T,3}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{T,k},\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(0,(\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\frac{dN_{t}^{k}}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{T,4}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{T},\mathbf{g}_{T,k}-(\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}})^{2}}{\lambda_{t}(f^{0}_{k})^{2}}R\bigg(\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(\xi_{T},\mathbf{g}_{T,k}-(\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg)dN_{t}^{k}.

Now, observe that for all
(l,k)\in[K]^{2}
, since
h^{0}_{l,k}\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|h^{0}_{l,k}+g_{T,l,k}|\mathds{1}_{h^{0}_{l,k}<-g_{T,l,k}}\leq|g_{T,l,k}|\mathds{1}_{h^{0}_{l,k}<-g_{T,l,k}}.

Moreover,
\arctan(x)=x\kappa(x)
with
\kappa(0)=1
and
\kappa
is in
[0,1]
. The arguments used to prove that
\bar{R}_{T,i}(\xi,\mathbf{g})=o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)
(
i\in[4]
) also show, with the two previous remarks, that
\bar{R}_{T,i}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})=o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)
(
i\in[4]
). For the sake of completeness, we present in detail the computations for
\bar{R}_{T,1}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})
, one can proceed similarly for the other terms. Let
k\in[K]
and
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{0}\bigg(\Big|\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}(0,(\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}})}{\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\big(dN_{t}^{k}-\lambda_{t}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})\big)\Big|>\epsilon\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}(0,(\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}+\mathbf{g}_{T,k})\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}})^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}\big(0,|\mathbf{g}_{k}.\kappa(a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}/\sqrt{T})|\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}}/\sqrt{T}\big)^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}\big(0,|\mathbf{g}_{k}|\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}}/\sqrt{T}\big)^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}\big(0,|\mathbf{g}|\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}}\big)^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg].

Furthermore,
arctan(0)=0
,
sgn(\arctan(x))=sgn(x)
,
\mathbf{g}_{T,l,k}
is bounded by
\pi/2a_{T}\rightarrow 0
and
g_{l,k}(x)\geq 0
when
h^{0}_{l,k}(x)=0
. Whence,
\mathds{1}_{h^{0}_{l,k}<-g_{T,l,k}}\xrightarrow{a.s.}0
, as
T\rightarrow+\infty
. Thus, by dominated convergence theorem,
\mathbb{E}_{0}\bigg[\frac{\tilde{\lambda}_{A}^{k}\big(0,|\mathbf{g}_{k}|\mathds{1}_{\mathbf{h}^{0}_{k}<-\mathbf{g}_{T,k}}\big)^{2}}{\lambda_{A}^{k}(f^{0}_{k})}\bigg]\xrightarrow{}0
as
T\rightarrow+\infty
and it proves that
R_{T,1}^{\prime}(\xi,\mathbf{g})=o_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}(1)
.
∎

Now, we prove Lemma
2.3
on the LAN expansion in model
\mathcal{P}_{R}
, using what we did before for the LAN expansion in model
\mathcal{P}
(Lemma
2.2
).

. We fix
\alpha>0
and we consider
\Omega_{T}
the event defined in Lemma
F.1
. As
\mathbb{P}_{0}(\Omega_{T})\rightarrow 1
it is enough to prove the LAN expansion on the event
\Omega_{T}
. Moreover, let
(\xi,\mathbf{g})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}\times L_{2,\mathbf{h}^{0}}
, on the event
\Omega_{T}
we have

\displaystyle\underset{k\in[K]}{\min}\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\inf}\lambda_{t}^{k}(\nu^{0}_{k}+\xi_{T,k},h^{0}_{k}+a_{T}^{-1}\arctan(a_{T}\mathbf{g}_{k}/\sqrt{T}))

\displaystyle\geq\underset{k\in[K]}{\min}\frac{\nu^{0}_{k}}{2}-\frac{K\pi}{2a_{T}}\times\underset{l\in[K]}{\max}\hskip 1.42271pt\underset{t\in[0,T]}{\sup}N^{l}([t-A,t)]\geq\underset{k\in[K]}{\min}\frac{\nu^{0}_{k}}{2}-\frac{K\pi C_{\alpha}\log(T)}{2a_{T}}\geq 0,

for
T
large enough (recall that
\log(T)/a_{T}\rightarrow 0
). In this model, the intensity along the path (
2.9
) directed by
(\xi,\mathbf{g})
is given by
\lambda_{t}^{k}\big((\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}_{R}}\big)=\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big((\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}_{R}}\big)_{+}
. But with the previous inequality, on
\Omega_{T}
for
T
large enough,
\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big((\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}_{R}}\big)_{+}=\tilde{\lambda}_{t}^{k}\big((\xi(T),\mathbf{g}(T))_{\mathcal{P}_{R}}\big)
. Whence, we can decompose the difference of intensities as in (
B.5
). Then, all the arguments that follow in the proof of Lemma
2.2
for the linear model
\mathcal{P}
are still valid and the LAN property is thus proved for the model
\mathcal{P}_{R}
.

Then, as in the model
\mathcal{P}
, the convolution theorem comes directly with the LAN property and the differentiability of the functional by applying Theorem 2.4 of
[
42
]
.
∎

C
Proofs of Section
3

C.1
Proofs of the posterior contraction results

In this section, we prove Proposition
3.1
on
L_{1}
posterior contraction and Theorem
3.1
on
L_{2}
posterior contraction. The results on the
L_{2}
tests used in the proof of Theorem
3.1
are presented in the following Section
C.2
. Recall the definition of
\varepsilon_{T}
and
J_{T}
in (
3.8
).

We verify hypotheses of proposition 3.5 of
[
58
]
(case 1). First, even though our prior is not supported on the sets of parameters
f
satisfying condition (C1bis) of
[
58
]
, but on the set
\mathcal{F}_{R}^{1}
; proposition 3.5 of
[
58
]
can still be applied, see
[
56
]
for a justification. Next, the condition given by equation (8) in
[
58
]
is true in our case since the functions
h^{0}_{l,k}
are non-negative. Then we have to verify the usual type of assumptions for posterior contraction, the so-called prior mass, sieves and entropy conditions.

We begin with the prior mass condition, we have to verify that
\Pi(B_{\infty}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}))\geq e^{-c_{2}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}
with
c_{2}
defined in
(P1)
and

\displaystyle B_{\infty}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}):=\big\{f:\forall(l,k)\in[K]^{2},\nu^{0}_{k}\leq\nu_{k}\leq\nu^{0}_{k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{T},h^{0}_{l,k}\leq h_{l,k}\leq h^{0}_{l,k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}\big\}.

Since
\Pi_{\nu}
has a positive continuous density on
[0,+\infty[^{K}
, there exists a constant
r>0
such that
\Pi_{\nu}\big(\nu:\|\nu-\nu^{0}\|_{\infty}\leq\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}\big)\geq r(2\bar{\varepsilon}_{T})^{K}
, this lower bound is greater than
\exp(-c_{2}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}/4)
for
T
large enough. Moreover, by assumption
(P1)
, (
3.4
) and (
3.7
) we have for
T
large enough:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{h}}}\big(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}:\forall(l,k)\in[K]^{2},\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}\leq\tilde{h}_{l,k}\leq\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}/L\big)

\displaystyle\geq\Pi_{\tilde{\mathbf{h}}|\bar{J}_{T}}\big(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}:\forall(l,k)\in[K]^{2},\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}\leq\tilde{h}_{l,k}\leq\tilde{h}^{0}_{l,k}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}/L\big)\Pi_{J}(J=\bar{J}_{T})

\displaystyle\geq e^{-c_{2}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}/2}e^{-2c_{1}\bar{J}_{T}\log(\bar{J}_{T})}\geq e^{-c_{2}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}/2}e^{-4c_{1}c_{3}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}\geq e^{-3c_{2}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}/4}

As
\varphi
is
L
-Lipschitz and non decreasing, it proves that
(\Pi_{\nu}\otimes\Pi_{\mathbf{h}})(B_{\infty}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}))\geq e^{-3c_{2}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}/4}
. Consequently, because for
T
large enough,
B_{\infty}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{T})\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{R}^{1}
, we have for
T
large enough
\Pi(B_{\infty}(\bar{\varepsilon}_{T}))\geq e^{-c_{2}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}
.

Next, we study the sieve condition. We define
\mathcal{\tilde{H}}_{T}:=\big\{\tilde{\mathbf{h}}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}B_{j},\hskip 2.84544pt\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{2}j},\hskip 2.84544ptj\leq J_{T},\hskip 2.84544pt\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq M_{T}\big\}
with
M_{T}
the sequence of assumption
(P1)
,
\mathcal{H}_{T}:=\big\{\varphi(\tilde{\mathbf{h}}),\tilde{\mathbf{h}}\in\mathcal{\tilde{H}}_{T}\big\}
and
\Upsilon_{T}:=\big\{\nu:\|\nu\|_{\infty}\leq e^{(c_{4}-1)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}/2}\big\}
. We have to verify that
\Pi_{\nu}(\Upsilon_{T}^{c})+\Pi_{\mathbf{h}}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{c})=o(e^{-(c_{4}-1)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}/2})
. For
\Pi_{\nu}
, by assumption we have for some
a>1
,
\Pi_{\nu}(\Upsilon_{T}^{c})\lesssim e^{-a(c_{4}-1)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}/2}=o(e^{-(c_{4}-1)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}/2})
. Then, with direct computations and conditions (
3.4
) and (
3.5
), we obtain that for
T
large enough
\Pi_{\mathbf{h}}(\mathcal{H}_{T}^{c})\leq e^{-U(M_{T})}+e^{-c_{1}J_{T}\log(J_{T})/2}
. But, by assumption
(P1)
,
U(M_{T})\geq c_{4}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}
and
c_{1}J_{T}\log(J_{T})/2\geq c_{1}J_{0}\bar{J}_{T}\log(T)/4\geq c_{4}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}
and therefore sieve condition is verified.

Finally, there is an entropy condition to be verified:
\log(\mathcal{N}(\zeta_{0}\varepsilon_{T},\mathcal{H}_{T},\|.\|_{1}))\leq x_{0}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}
for some
\zeta_{0}>0
and
x_{0}>0
. As for any
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}
,
\|h_{1}-h_{2}\|_{1}\leq L\|\tilde{h}_{1}-\tilde{h}_{2}\|_{1}\leq L\sqrt{A}\|\tilde{h}_{1}-\tilde{h}_{2}\|_{2}

it is enough to show that
\log(\mathcal{N}(\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{T},\mathcal{\tilde{H}}_{T},\|.\|_{2}))\leq x_{0}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}
for some
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}>0
. Then, using (
3.2
) and the usual formula for the entropy of a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{K^{2}j}
, we obtain that for some
c>0

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{T},\mathcal{\tilde{H}}_{T},\|.\|_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J_{T}}\mathcal{N}\big(\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{T},\{\tilde{\mathbf{h}}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{T}B_{j},\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{2}j},\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq M_{T}\},\|.\|_{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J_{T}}\mathcal{N}(c\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{T}\gamma(j)/\sqrt{j},\{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{2}j}:\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq M_{T}\},\|.\|_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J_{T}}\Big(\frac{M_{T}\sqrt{j}}{c\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\gamma(j)\varepsilon_{T}}\Big)^{K^{2}j}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{J_{T}}\Big(\max(\gamma(j),1/\gamma(j))\frac{M_{T}\sqrt{j}}{c\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{T}}\Big)^{K^{2}j}.

By (
3.2
),
\gamma(j)
is a monotone sequence. Assume that
\gamma(j)
is non decreasing. Then, either for some
j\geq 1
,
\gamma(j)\geq 1/\gamma(1)
(
1/\gamma
is non increasing) and for
T
large enough and
j\leq J_{T}
we have
\max(\gamma(j),1/\gamma(j))\leq\gamma(J_{T})
, or for all
j
,
\gamma(j)\leq 1/\gamma(1)
and
\max(\gamma(j),1/\gamma(j))\leq 1/\gamma(1)
. In both cases we have
\max(\gamma(j),1/\gamma(j))\leq\gamma(J_{T})+1/\gamma(1)
. If
\gamma(j)
is non increasing we obtain similarly that
\max(\gamma(j),1/\gamma(j))\leq\gamma(1)+1/\gamma(J_{T})
. Whence, we have
\max(\gamma(j),1/\gamma(j))\leq\gamma(1)+1/\gamma(J_{T})+\gamma(J_{T})+1/\gamma(1):=w_{T}
and by assumption
w_{T}
has at most polynomial growth. Thus, for some
C>0

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{T},\mathcal{\tilde{H}}_{T},\|.\|_{2})

\displaystyle\leq J_{T}\Big(w_{T}\frac{M_{T}\sqrt{J_{T}}}{c\zeta_{0}^{\prime}\varepsilon_{T}}\Big)^{K^{2}J_{T}}\leq e^{CJ_{T}\log(T)}.

By condition (
3.7
), we have that
J_{T}\log(T)\lesssim T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}
so for some
x_{0}>0
,
CJ_{T}\log(T)\leq x_{0}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}
and the entropy condition is verified. It concludes the proof of Lemma
3.1
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{C}_{T}=\big\{f:\|f-f^{0}\|_{2}\leq\log(T)\varepsilon_{T}\big\}
, we have to prove that
\Pi(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c}|N)\rightarrow 0
under
\mathbb{P}_{0}
. By proposition
3.1
, we have under
\mathbb{P}_{0}
,
\Pi(\mathcal{W}_{T}^{1}|N)\rightarrow 1
and thus it is enough to prove that
\Pi(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{W}_{T}^{1}|N)\rightarrow 0
under
\mathbb{P}_{0}
. We prove this following the well known strategy of
[
26
]
for posterior contraction. Before applying this strategy, we make some preliminary definitions and remarks.

When
\varphi
is the identity function, we set
\mathcal{F}_{T}=\mathcal{W}_{T}^{1}
. Let
\bar{r}_{T}:=\lceil 2DC_{1}J_{T}\varepsilon_{T}\rceil
, where
D
is defined in Lemma
C.3
and
C_{1}
in Proposition
3.1
, we then have
\mathcal{F}_{T}\subset\big\{f:\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}\leq\bar{r}_{T}\big\}
. Indeed, it holds trivially if
\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon_{T}
and otherwise, by Lemma
C.3
we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{1}\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{C_{1}\varepsilon_{T}\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{DC_{1}\varepsilon_{T}\sqrt{J_{T}}\big(\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}+\varepsilon_{T}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2DC_{1}\varepsilon_{T}\sqrt{J_{T}}\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}}.

Dividing both sides by
\sqrt{\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}}
, we find that
\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}\leq 2DC_{1}\varepsilon_{T}\sqrt{J_{T}}
and the inclusion is verified.

When
\varphi
is not the identity function, we define
\mathcal{F}_{T}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{T}=\big\{f\in\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T},\|\mathbf{h}\|_{\infty}\leq r_{T}\big\}\cap\big\{f:\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{2}\geq\log(T)r_{T}\varepsilon_{T}\hskip 4.26773pt\text{or}\hskip 4.26773pt\|\mathbf{h}-\mathbf{h}^{0}\|_{\infty}\leq G\big\}.

and we set in this case
\bar{r}_{T}:=\lceil 2Ar_{T}\rceil
, for
T
large enough
\mathcal{F}_{T}\subseteq\{f:\|f-f^{0}\|_{2}\leq\bar{r}_{T}\}
.

Now, whatever
\varphi
, for
r\geq\underline{r}_{T}:=\lfloor\log(T)\rfloor
, we let
\mathcal{F}_{T}(r):=\big\{f\in\mathcal{F}_{T},\|f-f^{0}\|_{2}\in[r\varepsilon_{T},(r+1)\varepsilon_{T}[\big\}
. Let
(f^{r,i})_{i\leq\mathcal{N}_{r}}
be the centering points of a covering of
\mathcal{F}_{T}(r)
in terms of metric
\|.\|_{2}
and with radius
\varepsilon_{T}(r+1)\Delta/(4\eta)
(
\Delta
and
\eta
are universal positive constants defined in Section
C.2
). With the entropy computations done in the proof of proposition
3.1
, we find that for some
x_{0}^{\prime}>0
,
\mathcal{N}_{r}\leq\exp(x_{0}^{\prime}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2})
. Finally, let

\displaystyle D_{T}=\int_{\mathcal{\bar{F}}}e^{L_{T}(f)-L_{T}(f^{0})}d\Pi(f),

and recall the events
\Omega_{\tau,T}
and
\Xi_{T}
defined in Section
C.2
and both observable. We are now ready to apply the strategy of
[
26
]
. First, we have the classical decomposition (see also equation (24) in
[
58
]
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}[\Pi(\mathcal{C}_{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}|N)]

(C.1)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{0}\big(\tilde{\Omega}_{T}\cap\{D_{T}\leq e^{-(c_{4}-1)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}\}\big)+e^{(c_{4}-1)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}\Pi\big(\mathcal{F}_{T}^{c}\cap\mathcal{W}^{1}_{T}\big)+\mathbb{P}_{0}(\tilde{\Omega}_{T}^{c}\cup\Xi_{T}^{c})

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{0}[\phi\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{\tau,T}}]+e^{(c_{4}-1)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}\sum_{r=\underline{r}_{T}}^{\bar{r}_{T}}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}(r)}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{f}\Big[(1-\phi)\mathds{1}_{\Xi_{T}\cap\Omega_{\tau,T}}\Big|\mathcal{G}_{0-}\Big]\Big]d\Pi(f).

with
\tilde{\Omega}_{T}\subset\Omega_{\tau,T}
defined by (25) in
[
58
]
and that verifies
\mathbb{P}_{0}(\tilde{\Omega}_{T}^{c})\rightarrow 0
and with

\displaystyle\phi=\underset{\underline{r}_{T}\leq r\leq\bar{r}_{T}}{\max}\hskip 2.84544pt\underset{i\leq\mathcal{N}_{r}}{\max}\hskip 2.84544pt\phi_{f^{r,i}},

where the tests
\phi_{f^{r,i}}
are defined in lemma
C.2
. The first term on the right hand side of (
C.1
) goes to
0
as
T
goes to
+\infty
by equation (26) in
[
58
]
. The second term is equal to
0
when
\varphi
is the identity function and when
\varphi
is not the identity function, we have by assumption
(P2)
that this term is term goes to
0
as
T
goes to
+\infty
. Then, for the third term on the right hand side of (
C.1
), we can apply Lemma
C.1
that gives that
\mathbb{P}_{0}(\Xi_{T}^{c})=o(1)
and thus
\mathbb{P}_{0}(\tilde{\Omega}_{T}^{c}\cup\Xi_{T}^{c})=o(1)
. It remains to deal with the two terms involving the test
\phi
. For the type I error term, by Lemma
C.2
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{0}[\phi\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{\tau,T}}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=\underline{r}_{T}}^{+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\underset{i\leq\mathcal{N}_{l}}{\max}\hskip 2.84544pt\phi_{f^{r,i}}\mathds{1}_{\Omega_{\tau,T}}\Big]\leq e^{x_{0}^{\prime}T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}\sum_{r=\underline{r}_{T}}^{+\infty}e^{-cT\varepsilon_{T}^{2}r}\lesssim e^{\big(x_{0}^{\prime}-c\log(T)\big)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}=o(1).

For the type II error term, Lemma
C.2
gives that

\displaystyle e^{(c_{4}-1)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}\sum_{r=\underline{r}_{T}}^{\bar{r}_{T}}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}(r)}\mathbb{E}_{0}\Big[\mathbb{E}_{f}\Big[(1-\phi)\mathds{1}_{\Xi_{T}\cap\Omega_{\tau,T}}\Big|\mathcal{G}_{0-}\Big]\Big]d\Pi(f)\lesssim e^{\big((c_{4}-1)-c\log(T)\big)T\varepsilon_{T}^{2}}=o(1).

This terminates the proof of Theorem
3.1
.
∎

C.2
On the tests for the
L_{2}
posterior contraction

We study in this section the "
L_{2}
